\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 5 Plotting}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Plotting}{}{Plotting}{}{}{plotting}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{plotting-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}\href{https://www.python.org/}{\linkhilite{Python}} \hyperlink{python}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 51}]}} Integrated Development Environment (IDE) (\href{https://thonny.org/}{\linkhilite{Thonny}} \hyperlink{thonny}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 52}]}} or \href{https://www.spyder-ide.org/}{\linkhilite{Spyder}} \hyperlink{spyder}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 53}]}} - Do not use Python IDLE)%
\item{}Optional: \href{https://colab.research.google.com/}{\linkhilite{Google Colab}} \hyperlink{colab}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 54}]}}%
\item{}Optional: \href{https://sheets.google.com/}{\linkhilite{Google Sheets}} \hyperlink{sheets}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 69}]}}%
\item{}Optional: \href{https://www.gnu.org/software/octave/}{\linkhilite{Octave}} \hyperlink{octave}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 68}]}}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{plotting-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}How to download and use a Python IDE (Thonny or Spyder is suggested. Do not use Python IDLE)%
\item{}How to install Python modules such as numpy and matplotlib%
\item{}Learn how to use Google Colab, Google Sheets and Octave if you choose to use those platforms%
\item{}Plot a simple function in Python, Octave or Sheets%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
As you learn Instrumentation throughout the semester, you will be tasked with creating computer programs on the Circuit Playground Express (CPX) or Arduino. The CPX itself has it's own RAM, CPU, HDD and many sensors while the Arduino is a simple microcontroller with no sensors, or harddrive. The CPX though is kind of like a mini computer! You can plug the CPX into your computer via USB and access the hard drive (HDD) from your own computer. When you program on the CPX you need to write programs on the CPX itself so that the mini computer can run the program you wrote. The CPX knows how to read multiple different languages but in this class the main languate will be the \href{https://www.python.org/}{\linkhilite{Python}} language which has been ported to the CPX and called \href{https://circuitpython.org/}{\linkhilite{CircuitPython}} \hyperlink{python}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 51}]}}\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Since many programs will be written in CircuitPython it is import to first learn how to program some things in Python. Python can be easily downloaded by itself but it's nice to get what's called an Integrated Development Environment (IDE). This way programs can be written on the computer for practice and better debugging. This chapter is all about getting Python and an IDE downloaded on the computer and learning how to use it. Extra sections have been written to briefly discuss Octave, Google Sheets and Google Colab as well. You can use any of those platforms to complete the assignment.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 5.1 Python IDEs}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Python IDEs}{}{Python IDEs}{}{}{plotting-3}
\begin{introduction}{}%
So which IDE is the best and which is recommended? Two IDEs are recommended for Python. They are explained in the next two sections. Either one is totally fine. If you just Google \textquotedblleft{}Python download\textquotedblright{} you will find a humongous list of editors (Scratch, Anaconda, Canopy, Eclipse, PyDev, etc). It's easy to get lost when searching for something so broad. Again, just using Spyder or Thonny.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.1.1 Thonny}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Thonny}{}{Thonny}{}{}{plotting-3-3}
Thonny - \href{https://thonny.org/}{\linkhilite{https:\slash{}\slash{}thonny.org}}\hyperlink{thonny}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 52}]}} is a great, easy, simple and lightweight IDE that has been around for years. Note that there is a 32 bit and 64 bit version. Which one is necessary? Well that depends on how many bits the computer has. This is a great thing to Google. Type the following: "do I have a 32 bit or 64 bit computer" into Google. It's more than likely that everyone reading this has a 64 bit computer but it's always a good idea to check. Learning the difference between 32 and 64 bit computers is the topic of another chapter (\hyperref[voltage]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{voltage}}}}). The figure below is a screenshot of what Thonny looks like with a sine graph plotted.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Sine Graph in Thonny}{plotting-3-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Thonny.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{subsectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.1.2 Spyder}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Spyder}{}{Spyder}{}{}{plotting-3-4}
Spyder - \href{https://www.spyder-ide.org/}{\linkhilite{https:\slash{}\slash{}www.spyder-ide.org\slash{}}}\hyperlink{spyder}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 53}]}} is a powerful IDE that is widely used in the scientific Python community. The figure below is a screenshot of what Spyder looks like with a sine graph plotted.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Sine Graph in Spyder}{plotting-3-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Spyder.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{subsectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.1.3 Setting up the IDE}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Setting up the IDE}{}{Setting up the IDE}{}{}{plotting-3-5}
Once Thonny or Spyder is installed, numpy and matplotlib are necessary modules within Python that allow Python to do some extra things like numerical computation with Python (numpy) and Matlab style Plotting libraries (matplotlib). How to install modules in Youtube is in the Extra Help section \hyperref[extra-help]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{extra-help}}}}; however, the simple way is to head over to Tools\textgreater{}Manage Packages in Thonny. The image below shows that version 3.1.2 is already installed but can be upgraded to 3.2.2%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Installing Matplotlib in Thonny}{plotting-3-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/IDE_upgrades.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
If numpy or matplotlib is not already included in Spyder then type the following into the Python Console in the lower right hand corner of Spyder which is called the IPython console.%
\begin{preformatted}
!pip install matplotlib
\end{preformatted}
If that doesn't work try%
\begin{preformatted}
!pip3 install matplotlib
\end{preformatted}
The output example below shows that matplotlib is already installed installed as it says \textquotedblleft{}requirement already satisfied\textquotedblright{}. Assuming there is a valid internet connection it will install the necessary module.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Installing Python Modules via Thonny Console}{plotting-3-5-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.25}{0.5}{0.25}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/console_thonny.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{subsectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.1.4 Scripting}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Scripting}{}{Scripting}{}{}{plotting-3-6}
Once numpy and matplotlib is installed it's time to make a plot. Here is a simple example.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Close up text of plotting in Python}{plotting-3-6-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.25}{0.5}{0.25}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/matplotlib_example.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code above will plot a sine wave from 0 to 2pi. The two lines at the top are importing the numpy and matplotlib modules you installed earlier. When they are imported we give them shorter names so it's easier to reference them so numpy will now be called np and matplotlib.pyplot will be called plt. The next two lines then create a vector \textquotedblleft{}x\textquotedblright{} from 0 to 2pi using 1000 data points. The next line then uses the sine function to create the vector \textquotedblleft{}y\textquotedblright{}. Finally \textquotedblleft{}x\textquotedblright{} and \textquotedblleft{}y\textquotedblright{} are plotted and the figure is instructed to pop up on your screen using the show() function.%
\end{subsectionptx}
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 5.2 Other Plotting Tools}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Other Plotting Tools}{}{Other Plotting Tools}{}{}{plotting-4}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.2.1 Google Colab}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Google Colab}{}{Google Colab}{}{}{plotting-4-2}
It is possible to use \href{https://colab.research.google.com}{\linkhilite{Google Colab}}\hyperlink{colab}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 54}]}} to collaborate on Python projects, run in the cloud\slash{}browser. In order to get Colab to work a Google account is necessary. Colab is a great tool for collaborating on Python projects and sharing code with others. It also has the advantage of running in the cloud so it can be accessed from any device with an internet connection. Although it's possible to head directly to the Colab website, it is recommended to open Colab through Google Drive. To do that simply open Google Drive\textgreater{}+New\textgreater{}More\textgreater{}Connect more apps and then install Google Colab as shown in the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Installing Google Colab through Google Drive}{plotting-4-2-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Google_Colab_Install.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once that's done it will be possible to open a new Google Colab notebook. The figure below is a screenshot of what Colab looks like with a sine graph plotted. The other benefit to using Google Colab is that Gemini is imbedded into Google Colab for easy AI integration.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Sine Graph in Google Colab}{plotting-4-2-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Colab.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower

\end{subsectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.2.2 Octave}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Octave}{}{Octave}{}{}{plotting-4-3}
Octave is very similar to MATLAB but free. The only major downside is that Octave is much slower and doesn't have a lot of extra utilities that MATLAB does. However, if it's not possible to get MATLAB, Octave is a great alternative. The figure below is a screenshot of what Octave looks like with a sine graph plotted.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Sine Graph in Octave}{plotting-4-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Octave.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Notice that the syntax is very similar to Python but slightly different. Deep diving into programming is not the focus of this class but anyone wanting to learn more about programming in Python or Octave can find plenty of resources online. The best way to learn is to just start writing code and then Google any errors that come up. The error messages are usually pretty descriptive and will lead to the answer. Furthermore, with the spawn of Generative AI many students often code better than ever before with a few prompts into any AI tool like Gemini, Copilot, Grok or ChatGPT. Also, the extra help section (xref ref="extra-help"\slash{}\textgreater{}) has many youtube videos on how to program if videos are preferred to AI or text.%
\end{subsectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.2.3 Google Sheets}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Google Sheets}{}{Google Sheets}{}{}{plotting-4-4}
Google Sheets is typically already installed in all browsers again provided a Google account is created first. However, Microsoft Excel can also be used if a Google account is not available. The figure below is a screenshot of what Google Sheets looks like with a sine graph plotted. Plotting in spreadsheet programs can be cumbersome but it is possible to do and is a good option for those who are not interested in learning Python or Octave or programming in general. Futhermore, there is a tongue-in-cheek comment from recently graduated engineers that no one knows how to code and everyone just uses Excel. So if that's the future of engineering, Google Sheets or Excel is the way to go.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Sine Graph in Google Sheets}{plotting-4-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Sheets.png}
\end{image}%

\end{subsectionptx}

\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 5.3 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{extra-help}
\begin{introduction}{}%
If extra help is needed with this assignment there is a \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qaxukpYRqfA\&list=PL_D7_GvGz-v1RsDs_OdNW65qRjEjmpfQx\&index=14}{\linkhilite{youtube video on how to install Thonny}} as well as a \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OjYwET-6QtE\&list=PL_D7_GvGz-v1RsDs_OdNW65qRjEjmpfQx\&index=35}{\linkhilite{youtube video on how to install Spyder}}. There is also a pretty \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7nAzHPURYW0\&list=PL_D7_GvGz-v1RsDs_OdNW65qRjEjmpfQx\&index=8}{\linkhilite{comprehensive youtube video on how to plot in matplotlib}} \hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Subsection 5.3.1 Built-In Help Function and dir()}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{subsectionptx}{Subsection}{Built-In Help Function and dir()}{}{Built-In Help Function and dir()}{}{}{extra-help-3}
Running code will always create syntax errors. Typing your syntax error into Google will yield so many results you might get lost. Sometimes it helps to know how to learn things just from your computer. For example, type in the commands below in the IPython console or the Shell.%
\par
\emph{import numpy as np}%
\par
\emph{dir(np)}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Output of dir(numpy)}{extra-help-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/dir.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
I included a photo of the output from the dir function. You'll notice there are a ton of functions in numpy. Every function in Python has a%
\begin{preformatted}
__doc__
\end{preformatted}
function. That's two underscores followed by \textquotedblleft{}doc\textquotedblright{} and then another two underscores. If you're ever curious about what a particular function does you can just run the command below again in the IPython console or Shell. In this example I'm looking at what \emph{arctan2} does.%
\begin{preformatted}
print(np.arctan2.__doc__)
\end{preformatted}
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Output of print(np.arctan2.\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}doc\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{})}{extra-help-3-10}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/arctan2_doc.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
You'll see that arctan2 takes 2 input arguments \textquotedblleft{}x1\textquotedblright{} and \textquotedblleft{}x2\textquotedblright{}. I didn't include the entire output but if you continue to scroll through the output it will even include examples on how to use the function.%
\par
Another way to learn certain functions is by visiting the appropriate documentation. The \href{https://numpy.org/doc/}{\linkhilite{Numpy Docs}} website for example has all the documentation you need for Numpy \hyperlink{python}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 51}]}}. Navigating that website you can find the same \href{https://numpy.org/doc/1.19/reference/generated/numpy.arctan2.html?highlight=arctan2\#numpy.arctan2}{\linkhilite{documentation for arctan2}}.%
\par
As a last resort you can always Google \textquotedblleft{}how to compute the inverse tangent 2 function in Python\textquotedblright{}. Note though that there is so much content out there on Google that you could easily get lost. Still, there's also so much information that the answers are out there for just about anything.%
\par
So you have three methods for finding out how to program in python. The dir and \emph{\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}doc\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}} functions in Python, using the appropriate documentation online and of course Google. I'm lumping Youtube in with Google which is also another way to learn information although when I want to find information quickly I just use the documentation. It's the best in my opinion.%
\end{subsectionptx}
\end{sectionptx}

%

\typeout{Section 5.4 Assignment}

\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{plotting-6}
\begin{introduction}{}%
The assignment for this project is to plot the equation below from 0 to 10 seconds and include that Figure in a report. The Figure must include a grid and an x-axis label (‘Time (sec)') and y-axis label (‘Temperature (F)'). Any plotting tool Sheets, Excel, Colab, Python, Octave or similar is acceptable provided the figure conforms to the requirements above and the specific rubric below.%
\begin{equation}
T(t) = 60(1-e^{-5t})+30\label{plotting-6-2-1-1}
\end{equation}
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire screen) of your plotting environment with your code pasted into the workspace - 40\%%
\item{}Include the plot of temperature vs time being sure to save the figure so it is in high resolution - 40\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen running the example code and showing the result of \emph{plt.show()} - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
%
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}

\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 6 Setting up your Microcontroller}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Setting up your Microcontroller}{}{Setting up your Microcontroller}{}{}{getting-started}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{getting-started-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}\href{https://codewith.mu/en/download}{\linkhilite{Mu}} \hyperlink{mu}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 57}]}} or \href{https://www.arduino.cc/en/software/}{\linkhilite{Arduino IDE}} \hyperlink{Arduino}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 35}]}}%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB \href{https://circuitpython.org}{\linkhilite{UF2 File}} \hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB or Arduino Family (UNO, MEGA, or similar)%
\item{}USB Cable (with a data line. Not all USB cables have data lines)%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{getting-started-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Learn how to setup your Microcontroller%
\item{}Learn how to install Mu or Arduino IDE%
\item{}Learn how to code in CircuitPython or Arduino%
\item{}Learn about hardware and software debugging%
\item{}Learn the difference between Python and CircuitPython and potentially Arduino%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
By now you hopefully have your Circuit Playground Express (CPX), Circuit Playground Bluefruit (CPB) or an Arduino and it's time to get your microcontroller up and running. A very in depth and detailed tutorial can be found on the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/circuitpython-made-easy-on-circuit-playground-express/first-things-first}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Learn site}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}} for the CPX and similar tutorials exists for the Arduino. This chapter is a summary of what you need to do to get the CPX or Arduino up and running as well as writing a simple blink code.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 6.1 Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{}{getting-started-setup}
When you get your CPX and plug it into the computer via USB it actually won't run CircuitPython just yet. First you need to double click the reset button (the button in the center. It says RESET above the button) and put it into boot mode. All the neopixels (the ring lights on the CPX) will light up green.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{The Circuit Playground Express in Boot mode}{getting-started-setup-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CPX_boot.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Something called CPLAYBOOT will pop up on your computer just like a USB stick or external harddrive. A couple files with be in there but it doesn't matter what they say right now.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Contents of the CPLAYBOOT Folder}{getting-started-setup-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CPLAYBOOT.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
You then need to download what's called a \href{https://circuitpython.org/board/circuitplayground_express/}{\linkhilite{UF2 file}} and transfer it onto the CPX. Note if you purchased a kit with the Bluefruit you need to download a \href{https://circuitpython.org/board/circuitplayground_bluefruit/}{\linkhilite{different UF2}}\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Make sure you get the right one. Once the UF2 is downloaded you need to drag the UF2 over to the CPLAYBOOT drive on your computer. After a bit of time a USB drive called CIRCUITPY will pop up as a flash drive on your computer. The CPX is now like a USB stick with 2MB of storage. \alert{Note that if you have an old bootloader you must update the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-bluefruit/update-bootloader-use-command-line}{\linkhilite{bootloader}} \hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}.}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Contents of the CIRCUITPY Folder}{getting-started-setup-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CIRCUITPY.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
At this point the CPX is like a mini computer. If you put CircuitPython code on the \textquotedblleft{}flash drive\textquotedblright{} it will run the code. Since I've done this before, there are a number of files already on my CPX. You may have some or none of these. The \textquotedblleft{}boot\textunderscore{}out.txt\textquotedblright{} file will tell you the version of CircuitPython you have on the CPX. Mine says this:%
\begin{preformatted}
Adafruit CircuitPython 5.3.0 on 2020-04-29; Adafruit CircuitPlayground Express with samd21g18
\end{preformatted}
Which means I have CircuitPython version 5.3.0 last updated on April 29th, 2020. The CPX itself is using the \href{https://www.microchip.com/wwwproducts/en/ATsamd21g18}{\linkhilite{ATSAMD21G18}} microprocessor \hyperlink{CPXchip}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 58}]}}. The folder lib is a folder with extra libraries that you may need to install later. The file main.py is the CircuitPython file that the CPX is currently running. \alert{The CPX can store as many CircuitPython files as possible for a 2MB flash drive but it will only run ONE CircuitPython script at a time and that file must be named main.py} The folder \emph{System\textunderscore{}Volume\textunderscore{}Information} is a file management folder that we will never use.%
\par
If you want the CPX to run code you simply need to edit the file \emph{main.py} or if that file does not exist you just need to create it. You could just open Notepad or any other text app (Sublime,TexWrangler, Emacs, Vi, Nano, Gedit, Notepad++, Wordpad, VSCode, etc) but the CPX has alot of debugging options and it is recommended to use a program called \textquotedblleft{}\href{https://codewith.mu/en/download}{\linkhilite{Mu}}\textquotedblright{}\hyperlink{mu}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 57}]}}. Mu is a good way to write and debug code on the CPX. Note that Mu is only used to program the CPX. If you want to run Python code on your laptop you need to use Thonny or Spyder (or whatever other IDE you downloaded). If you want to run CircuitPython code on your CPX you must use Mu. Once you've downloaded and installed Mu and open it up it will look like this (Note it's possible the the software gets updated from the time this book is published. As such be sure to select the CircuitPython Mode for board development).%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Opening Screen for Mu}{getting-started-setup-12}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Mu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Make sure to select the \textquotedblleft{}Adafruit CircuitPython\textquotedblright{} option. If the software has been updated and that option no longer exists, be sure to select the option that says CircuitPython for board development.%
\par
Alright, let's start writing code! If you have a file called \emph{main.py} on your CPX click the Load button and load \emph{main.py} (make sure to load the main.py that is stored on your CPX and not somewhere else on your computer.) If a file \emph{main.py} does not exist on your CPX simply click the New button and then Save the file as \emph{main.py} (again make sure you save it to the CPX and not to your computer)%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Opening main.py in Mu}{getting-started-setup-15}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Mu2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
You'll see that I am accessing the CIRCUITPY drive and saving the file as main.py The file itself is empty and just has a comment using the \# symbol. At this point since the file is blank the CPX won't do anything.%
\par
\alert{Now this is really important. Your CPX is a USB stick that can hold as many CircuitPython files as 2MB will allow but it can only run or execute one CircuitPython script at a time. Furthermore it will only run two types of files. It will run code.py if it exists and if it can't find code.py it will run main.py If the CPX can't find main.py or code.y it will just not do anything. If you have two versions of main.py or a combination of main.py or code.py it will run one of them and not the other. Make sure you only have one version of main.py or code.by but not both! Some common things to check if your CPX isn't working.}%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Make sure you're using Mu in the right Mode%
\item{}Make sure main.py or code.py is on the CIRCUITPY drive and not somewhere on your computer.%
\item{}Make sure you are editing the right file in Mu. Do you have two versions of main.py?%
\item{}Are you editing using Thonny or Spyder?%
\item{}Are you editing a file on your computer? Make sure you are writing to the CIRCUITPY drive.%
\item{}Unplug the CPX, close Mu and try again.%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
So let's get the CPX to do something simple like blink an LED. I have an entire \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers}{\linkhilite{Github}} devoted to Microelectronics. Specifically I have a \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/tree/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{folder with all of my Circuit Playground files}}. The easiest program to run is the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/blink/blink.py}{\linkhilite{blink.py}} script. I've attached a screenshot of the script below\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython Blink Code for the Circuit Playground Express}{getting-started-setup-19}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/blink.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
We will talk about what this code is doing later on. For now copy and paste these 17 or so lines of code and paste them into Mu specifically the \emph{main.py} script. It will hopefully look like this.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Blink Code inside Mu}{getting-started-setup-21}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/blinkmu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Make sure to save. You can click \emph{Zoom-In} and \emph{Zoom-out} to zoom in and out to change the font size and see more of the output. If the blink code is working you will see a red led labeled D13 on the CPX blink back and forth. D13 stands for digital pin 13. You'll notice there are analog pins labeled A5 and A6 among other numbers. Let's talk about the code a bit more and explain why it's doing what it's doing. The three lines at the top of the code are \emph{import} commands to import different modules just like we did for \emph{numpy} and \emph{matplotlib}. In this case the modules being imported are \emph{board}, \emph{digitalio} and \emph{time}. The module \emph{board} is used to import the layout of the CPX so we can access different pins on the CPX. The module \emph{digitalio} stands for digital input output which means we are inputting and outputting digital signals. Since we can combine this module with the \emph{board} module we will be able to output digital signals to different pins on the CPX board. Remember that PCB stands for printed circuit board so \emph{board} implies we are accessing pins on the CPX PCB. Hopefully that makes sense. The final module we are importing is \emph{time} which acts just like the \emph{time} module on your desktop computer. It will let us access the CPX's internal clock.%
\par
Moving along, lines 7 and 8 create an LED object using the \emph{board} module and \emph{digitalio}. It's a long line of code that basically says, create a variable called \emph{led} that lets us output a digital signal to pin D13. We also set the direction of the LED to output since we only want to write to the LED.%
\par
Lines 10 through 19 kick off an infinite loop that never ends. The line that says \emph{while True:} means loop while \emph{True}. Well \emph{True} is always true which means it will loop forever. The colon at the end of the line tells CircuitPython that the loop condition statement ends and to begin looping from 11 through 19.%
\par
Line 11 specifically says \emph{print(time.monotonic())}. First the \emph{print()} function is used to print things so that you and I can see it. Rather than just seeing a blinking LED we want to see the time printed. The \emph{time.monotonic()} is using the module \emph{time} which we imported and using a function from that module called \emph{monotonic()} which calls the internal clock of the CPX. So how do you see the output from the print statement? Hit the \emph{Serial} button on Mu and you will hopefully see some output. Here's what it looks like on my machine.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial Monitor Open in Mu}{getting-started-setup-26}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/blinkmu2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case you can see the time printed every time it goes through the loop. You may even see an error. This \emph{Serial} button is great for debugging because it will tell you the error in your code. For example, in the picture below I have an error in my code and the \emph{Serial} output is letting me know.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Error Shown in Serial Monitor}{getting-started-setup-28}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/blinkerror.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case I have an error on line 5. It's saying there is no module named \emph{timed}. The reason that module doesn't exist is because the module is actually \emph{time} not \emph{timed}. You can use the \emph{Serial} monitor to check on your program and see what errors you may have. Ok so there are two more lines of code to discuss. They are \emph{led.value = True} and \emph{time.sleep(0.5)}.%
\par
These lines of code are repeated throughout the while loop and do two things. First, the \emph{led.value} either sets the value of the LED to True which turns the light on or False which turns the light off. The LED is digital which means the signal can either be on or off. There's no in between for digital signals. The \emph{time.sleep()} function tells the CPX to pause for half a second. You can change the number in the parentheses if you want to change length of time the code pauses. Note that the CPX completely pauses. That is no code runs during a sleep.%
\par
If you've gotten the LED to blink you're all set for this lab. However, I'd like to you learn a few more things about documentation. Just like Python on your desktop you can lookup the documentation on the CPX itself. For example, I've added a print statement to print the directory of time.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Showing Directory of the time module in Mu}{getting-started-setup-32}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/printtimeMu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case I've added \emph{print(dir(time))} to line 7. The output shows that the \emph{time} module has 9 functions including \emph{monotonic()}. Unfortunately CircuitPython does not have \emph{\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}doc\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}} functions built in which means if you want to learn about a specific function, you need to visit the \href{https://circuitpython.readthedocs.io/en/5.3.x/README.html}{\linkhilite{documentation for CircuitPython}}. Here's the \href{https://circuitpython.readthedocs.io/en/latest/shared-bindings/time/index.html}{\linkhilite{specific documentation for the}} \href{https://circuitpython.readthedocs.io/en/latest/shared-bindings/time/index.html}{\linkhilite{time}} \href{https://circuitpython.readthedocs.io/en/latest/shared-bindings/time/index.html}{\linkhilite{module}}. A lot of \href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_Learning_System_Guides/tree/master/CircuitPython_Essentials}{\linkhilite{example code from the Adafruit Learning System is also on Github}}\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}.%
\par
Finally, in order to keep practicing using Python on your desktop I want you to modify the code above to run on your desktop computer. You're going to have to modify a few things. First, make sure to open Thonny or Spyder depending on which version of Python IDE you downloaded. Then, only import the \emph{time} module. All the other modules don't exist on your desktop. Also, get rid of all the lines of code that blink the LED. We just want to print time in a while loop. Finally, the time module on your desktop uses a function called time.time() (unless you have Python3 installed) so when you print time make sure to use that module instead. Again visit the \href{https://docs.python.org/3/library/time.html\#functions}{\linkhilite{documentation for time for Python if you want to learn more}}\hyperlink{python}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 51}]}}. Thonny by default will load Python version 3 but it's possible you may have Python version 2 so make sure you look up the documentation for the appropriate version of Python. After searching through the documentation you can use a function called asctime() on your desktop. This is the output I get in Thonny when I add a sleep of 1 second. The exercise for you is to do something similar.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Output of datetime in Thonny}{getting-started-setup-35}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/blinkcomputer.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 6.2 TL;DR \slash{} Extra Help for the CPX\slash{}CPB}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{TL;DR \slash{} Extra Help for the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{TL;DR \slash{} Extra Help for the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{}{getting-started-4}
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Plug in your CPX and double tap the reset button to go into reset mode. CPLAYBOOT will mount to your computer. (Some CPXes you need to hold the button down instead of double tap).%
\item{}Download the \href{https://circuitpython.org/downloads}{\linkhilite{UF2 file}}\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}.%
\item{}Drag the UF2 file to your CPLAYBOOT drive. After a few seconds CIRCUITPY will mount.%
\item{}You need to then download \href{https://codewith.mu/en/download}{\linkhilite{Mu}}\hyperlink{mu}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 57}]}}.%
\item{}Open Mu and make sure to select the mode Adafruit CircuitPython (or just CircuitPython if you have a new version)%
\item{}Open main.py (or code.py) in Mu from the CIRCUITPY drive in Mu. (It's possible you have code.py on your drive instead of main.py which is fine. Just open one or the other)%
\item{}Copy the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/blink/blink.py}{\linkhilite{blink.py}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}} script into main.py (or code.py)%
\end{enumerate}
There is an accompanying \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XFvLn6rwm3I}{\linkhilite{youtube video to help you see me perform the 8 steps above}}\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 6.3 Setting up an Arduino}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setting up an Arduino}{}{Setting up an Arduino}{}{}{getting-started-5}
Setting up the Arduino is a bit different than the CPX\slash{}CPB family of microcontrollers. The main difference is that the code is done on your computer in the Arduino IDE and then sent or compiled to the Arduino. This means that the code from the Arduino is unrecoverable if you don't have the code on your computer. It's also a lot harder to debug code on the Arduino because it isn't like CircuitPython where the language will tell you which line of code is incorrect. My strategy with coding on the Arduino is pretty archaic in that I like to add one line of code at a time and have a lot of print statements to see where the code fails. This won't solve memory leaks or hardware issues but it does help a bit. In this section we will dive into getting the blink code to work. The pro of using an Arduino is highlighted here in that the only setup is in the IDE itself. To start you just plug in your Arduino to your laptop and then open the Arduino IDE.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino IDE Setup}{getting-started-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/ArduinoIDE_Setup.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
When you open the Arduino IDE for the first time the window will open up with the \emph{void setup()} routine and the \emph{void loop()} routine. The \emph{setup} routine runs once and is where all the one time setup code goes. The \emph{loop} routine runs over and over again and never stops and unlike the CPX\slash{}CPB you can never stop it unless you have a line of code that stops the code automatically. To start programming on your microcontroller you first need to select the Tools\textgreater{}\emph{Board, Processor} and \emph{Port}. The \emph{Board} depends on the kind of board you purchased. For example, I purchased the Arduino MEGA so I selected Mega for the Board and Processor but you might have to select UNO if you're using an Arduino UNO. The \emph{Port} is the serial port that the Arduino is connected to. Since I'm on Linux the port the Arduino is connected to is \emph{\slash{}dev\slash{}ttyACMO} while it would be \emph{COM3} or something similar on Windows or \emph{\slash{}dev\slash{}tty.usbmodem14101} on Mac. This is shown in the Figure above. When you're ready to code the blink example the easy part is that the code for that is already included in the examples in the Arduino IDE. To do that click \emph{File\textgreater{}Examples\textgreater{}01.Basics\textgreater{}Blink}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino Blink Example Using Pin 13}{getting-started-5-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/ArduinoBlink_13.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The figure above shows the code for blinking the LED connected to pin 13. The Arduino UNO\slash{}MEGA and also the CPX\slash{}CPB all share the same default LED on pin 13. The default example program will say \emph{LED\textunderscore{}BUILTIN} but I changed the pin to pin 13 to be more specific. The code is pretty straightforward where the setup routine makes pin 13 an output pin using the \emph{pinMode()} function and then the loop routine sets pin 13 to HIGH and LOW using the \emph{digitalWrite()} function with a \emph{delay} function used to pause for 1000 ms or 1 second. In this case HIGH is on and LOW is off. If you click the \emph{Upload} button which looks like a right arrow the code will be compiled to binary and then uploaded to the Arduino. If it's working properly the LED on pin 13 will blink.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA with Pin 13 Illuminated}{getting-started-5-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_MEGA_Blink.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Again there isn't a lot of ability to debug if it isn't working right. Note that if you want to save this code you must click Save and then save the \emph{.ino} file to somewhere on your computer. If you want to share code you can't just give someone your Arduino, you have to send them the code because the code is unrecoverable from the binary format on the Arduino itself. As for the code itself, the \slash{}\slash{} is used for comments and semicolons ; are used to end every single line. Unlike python you don't need to worry about tabs to be setup properly. Loops and if statements are separated by brackets \textbraceleft{} and \textbraceright{}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino Blink Example with Serial Monitor}{getting-started-5-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_Blink_Serial.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
As I mentioned however, it's possible to add many print statements to the code and open up the Serial Monitor. The figure above shows the edited code with \emph{Serial.begin(9600)} added to the setup routine which opens up serial communication between your computer and the Arduino at a baud rate of 9600 bits per second. In the loop routine I have added many \emph{Serial.print("");} commands to print to the Serial monitor which can be opened by clicking the button that looks like a magnifying glass. Make sure the baud rate in the serial monitor is set to the same 9600 value or the values will be gibberish. Also \emph{millis()} is the internal timer on all Arduinos that output time in milliseconds. Note because this program is so simple, the code is left to the student to recreate.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 6.4 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{getting-started-6}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you must appropriately set up your CPX\slash{}CPB, get the blink code to run and include some screenshots (your entire computer screen) of relevant steps. \alert{Note for the blink code, modify blink pattern to be different from the default on Github}. The specific items requested are shown below.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of your computer showing the CPLAYBOOT drive on your computer - 20\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of your computer showing the CIRCUITPY drive on your computer - 20\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu with the Serial monitor open showing the output of the blink code. Remember to modify the blink pattern - 20\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your CPX\slash{}CPB with the red led on - 20\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself showing the CPX\slash{}CPB blinking and your entire screen with your blink code running with the Serial monitor open - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used on your CPX\slash{}CPB and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 7 Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Help}{}{Help}{}{}{troubleshooting}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
Are you getting tons of errors because a certain moduble is not installed? Is your CPX\slash{}CPB broken or not running code? This chapter walks through a few common mishaps that will happen as you work through these code examples.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.1 Updating your Bootloader}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Updating your Bootloader}{}{Updating your Bootloader}{}{}{troubleshooting-3}
Some of the newer CircuitPython UF2s do not install correctly on old boot loaders. If you put the CPX\slash{}CPB into CPLAYBOOT mode and open the \emph{INFO\textunderscore{}UF2.TXT} file it will tell you the version of the bootloader that you have just like the figure below. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Old Bootloader Version}{old-bootloader}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/old_bootloader.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 In this case the version of the bootloader is 0.2.11 which is older than most new UF2s require. If you're having an issue with an old bootloader be sure to update the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-bluefruit/update-bootloader-use-command-line}{\linkhilite{bootloader for the CPB}} or alternatively the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-express/updating-the-bootloader}{\linkhilite{bootloader for the CPX}}. At the time of this writing here is the command you need to upload the new bootloader the CPB. If you have version 0.4.0 or later you can use the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-bluefruit/update-bootloader-use-uf2}{\linkhilite{UF2 method}} \hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. If your bootloader is too old you need \emph{adafruit-nrfutil}. Here is the command you need to upload the new bootloader to the CPB.\begin{preformatted}
adafruit-nrfutil --verbose dfu serial --package 
circuitplayground_nrf52840_bootloader-0.8.2_s140_6.1.1.zip -p /dev/ttyACM0 
-b 115200 --singlebank --touch 1200
\end{preformatted}
\terminology{For windows users} you use the \emph{adafruit-nrfutil.exe} file from the Adafruit bootloader website. Then, usingcommand prompt to run the command above you need to change the tty port to the correct COM port which can be found using \emph{Device Manager}.%
\par
\terminology{For linux users} you will need a few things%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}You need pip3 \(->\) \textdollar{} sudo apt install python3-pip%
\item{}You need adafruit-nrfutil \(->\) \textdollar{} pip3 install -{}-{}user adafruit-nrfutil%
\item{}adafruit-nrfutil gets installed into \textbackslash{}textasciitilde\slash{}.local\slash{}bin%
\item{}You need to add your user to dialout \(->\) \textdollar{} sudo usermod -a -G your\textunderscore{}username%
\item{}Restart your computer%
\end{enumerate}
This is what it looks like when running the \emph{adafruit-nrfutil} command on linux. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Output from running the bootloader update command on linux}{troubleshooting-3-3-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/bootloader_linux_command.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 For the CPX there is a UF2 that you transfer over to the CPLAYBOOT drive. At the time of this writing the version you're looking for is \emph{update-bootloader-circuitplay\textunderscore{}m0-v3.15.0.uf2}. Once you update the bootloader you can check the version by opening the \emph{INFO\textunderscore{}UF2.TXT} file again and it should show the new version. The figure below shows an example of the new bootloader version after updating. In this case the version is 0.10.0 which is much newer than the old 0.2.11 version. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Updated Bootloader Version}{troubleshooting-3-3-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/updated_bootloader.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.2 Installing Modules}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Installing Modules}{}{Installing Modules}{}{}{installing-modules}
\alert{One issue you're going to run into when you run certain codes is that you won't have some of the modules on your CPB\slash{}CPX.} To fix this you need to download the \href{https://circuitpython.org/libraries}{\linkhilite{CircuitPython Libraries}}\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. You need to download the appropriate version: 6.x, 7.x or 8.x. The figure below is a screenshot of the libraries homepage on the CircuitPython website. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython Libraries homepage}{installing-modules-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/libraries.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 How do you know what version of CircuitPython you have? Well head over to your CIRCUITPY drive and open the \emph{boot\textunderscore{}out.txt} file and it will tell you the version. The Figure below shows an example \emph{boot\textunderscore{}out.txt} file. In this case the version is 10.1.4 so, you would download the 10.x version of the libraries. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example boot\textunderscore{}out.txt file showing the version of CircuitPython}{installing-modules-2-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/example_boot_out.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 Note that this is the same version as the .UF2 file installed back in the Getting Started labs (See \hyperref[getting-started]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{getting-started}}}}). When you download the modules it will download a .zip file. Extract the .zip file on your desktop computer and then open the \emph{lib} folder on your desktop and your CIRCUITPY. You then need to transfer the modules (ONLY THE ONES YOU NEED) from your desktop to your CPX\slash{}CPB \emph{lib} folder. The reason why you can't copy the entire folder is because the CPB\slash{}CPX only has 2MB of flash and the CircuitPython download is 4.1 MB at the time of this writing. If your CIRCUITPY drive does not have a \emph{lib} folder you can easily create one as shown in the Figure below. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CIRCUITPY Drive showing lib folder}{installing-modules-2-12}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CIRCUITPY.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
%
\par
After extracting the zip folder to your computer you can begin dragging files over to your CPX\slash{}CPB. The Figure below shows an example lib folder full of modules that have been dragged from the desktop to the CIRCUITPY drive. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example lib folder on CIRCUITPY drive with modules dragged over from desktop}{installing-modules-3-1}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/lib_folder_full.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 The follow up question of course is \emph{Which modules do I need?} The answer to that question is in the code. When you run a code that needs a specific module, the Serial monitor will say \emph{ImportError: No module named 'x'}. In this case you will then know to install the module 'x'. You can also look at the import statements at the top of the code to see which modules are being imported and then make sure you have those modules installed. The figure below is an example of that error where the code is specifically looking for the module named \emph{adafruit\textunderscore{}circuitplayground}. In this case, the user would need to copy that specific module from the desktop to the CIRCUITPY drive \emph{lib} folder. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example of a missing module error}{moduleerror}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/missing_module.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 Note that it's possible to import too many modules on the CPX\slash{}CPB and run out of memory. It turns out that the CPB has much more memory then the CPX. Run the commands below to see how much memory your CPX\slash{}CPX has left.\begin{preformatted}
import gc
gc.collect()
print(gc.mem_free())
\end{preformatted}
At the time of this writing, the CPX has about 17 KB of RAM and the CPB has around 140 KB of RAM. Note that RAM is different than flash. The CPX\slash{}CPB can hold about 2MB of data but only operate with 17\slash{}140 KB of working memory.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.3 Reformatting your CPX\slash{}CPB}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Reformatting your CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{Reformatting your CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{}{troubleshooting-5}
If your CPX\slash{}CPX is completely bricked you can completely reformat it. In order to do that you simply put the CPX\slash{}CPB into CPLAYBOOT mode. Then drag either the \href{https://cdn-learn.adafruit.com/assets/assets/000/048/745/original/flash_erase_express.ino.circuitplay.uf2?1512152080}{\linkhilite{CPX eraser tool}} or the \href{https://cdn-learn.adafruit.com/assets/assets/000/082/950/original/CP_Bluefruit_QSPI_Erase.UF2?1572026649}{\linkhilite{CPB eraser tool}}. After that, you can reinstall the CircuitPython UF2 and start fresh (See \hyperref[getting-started]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{getting-started}}}}) \hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. If you have a linux computer you can also access the REPL of your CPX\slash{}CPB using the commands below\begin{preformatted}
sudo screen /dev/ttyACM0
import storage
storage.erase_filesystem()
\end{preformatted}
Then you would proceed with installing the CircuitPython UF2 again and starting fresh. Note that this will erase everything on your CPX\slash{}CPB so make sure you have a backup of your code and files before doing this. If you feel like you've tried everything this \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-express/troubleshooting}{\linkhilite{troubleshooting guide}} may have some other steps outlined to help you. However, I have had great experience with the eraser tool above \hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.4 Using an I2C Scanner}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Using an I2C Scanner}{}{Using an I2C Scanner}{}{}{I2C_scanner}
If you're having trouble getting an I2C device to work, it's a good idea to run an I2C scanner to make sure you can see the device and that you have the correct address. Here is a simple I2C scanner code that you can run on your CPX\slash{}CPB.%
\begin{preformatted}
import time
import board
import busio

i2c = busio.I2C(board.SCL, board.SDA)
while not i2c.try_lock():
    pass

try:
  while True:
      print("I2C addresses found:", [hex(device_address) for device_address in i2c.scan()])
  time.sleep(2)
\end{preformatted}
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.5 Common Mishaps}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Common Mishaps}{}{Common Mishaps}{}{}{troubleshooting-7}
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Your CPX is a USB stick that can hold as many CircuitPython files as 2MB will allow but it can only run or execute one CircuitPython script at a time. It will look for code.py and then main.py and that's it.%
\item{}Make sure you're using Mu in the right Mode (CircuitPython)%
\item{}Make sure main.py or code.py is on the CIRCUITPY drive and not somewhere on your computer.%
\item{}Make sure you are editing the right file in Mu. Do you have two versions of main.py or perhaps main.py and code.py?%
\item{}Do you have boot.py on there when you don't need it?%
\item{}Are you editing using Thonny or Spyder? You're supposed to use Mu.%
\item{}Are you editing a file on your computer? Make sure you are writing to the CIRCUITPY drive.%
\end{enumerate}
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 7.6 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{troubleshooting-8}
I have a youtube video where I \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xVMPgU5z9gE}{\linkhilite{update the bootloader on a CPB}}\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}} That may help you if you're having trouble following the bootloader tutorial above.%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 8 External LEDs and Push Buttons}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{External LEDs and Push Buttons}{}{External LEDs and Push Buttons}{}{}{led-buttons}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{led-buttons-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX + USB Cable%
\item{}2 Alligator clips%
\item{}Push Button%
\item{}Breadboard%
\item{}LED (Light Emitting Diode) (x3 in case you fry some)%
\item{}Resistor (300 to 1000 Ohms)%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{led-buttons-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}The VOUT and 3.3V pins are always "ON" even when code is not running on the CPX. So long as your CPX is plugged in via USB or a Lipo Battery%
\item{}LEDs are Light Emitting \emph{Diodes} which means current only flows in one direction%
\item{}LEDs need resistors in series otherwise they will get too hot and burn up%
\item{}Breadboard pinout diagrams%
\item{}Analog pins can be controlled by simply using the digitalio module%
\item{}LEDs can be hooked up to analog pins and set to blink by changing the board pin%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
In this project we're going to use the same blink code as Chapter \hyperref[getting-started]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{getting-started}}}} but modify it to blink an external LED. Another goal of this lab is to familiarize yourself with some of the pins on the CPX and create a simple circuit using the 5V (VOUT) pin on the CPX and one of the Analog pins.%
\par
Your laptop has a battery with something between 10 to 20V. There are DC to DC converters in your laptop that provide 5V to your USB ports. These USB ports can be used to power your CPX as you have done in the past few labs.%
\par
If you purchased the optional \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/3287}{\linkhilite{battery pack}} you can also power the CPX using 3 AA batteries \hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. These batteries nominally have 1.5 V but fully charged it's actually something like 1.8 V. So 1.8 times 3 is 5.4V which is enough to power the CPX. If you have the battery pack and some AA batteries, give it a try. If you still have the blink code from the last project on board you'll see the D13 LED blink as before. You won't be able to see the serial print() output as before but that code will be running which is why D13 is blinking. \alert{I have noticed that some of the battery packs have power and ground wires swapped. If the battery pack doesn't work it may be because those two wires are backwards.}%
\par
The CPX itself uses 3.3V logic which means when it converts numbers to binary a 0 (False) represents 0 volts and a 1 (True) represents 3.3V. The CPX has ports that are labeled various things. GND stands for ground and you need to hook the negative end of your circuit to this and it also has VOUT which supplies 5V to any circuit you build. Hook the positive end of your circuit to the VOUT pin. There is also a port labeled 3.3V and obviously that outputs 3.3V. The Figure below shows an \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/circuitpython-digital-inputs-and-outputs/digital-outputs }{\linkhilite{Arduino Metro}} hooked up to an LED on a breadboard (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/tdicola}{\linkhilite{Tony Dicola}} \hyperlink{Metro}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 37}]}}).%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Fritzing diagram of an Arduino Metro hooked up to an LED \hyperlink{Metro}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 37}]}}}{led-buttons-2-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/circuitpython_01_digital_io_figure_1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Your lab today specifically involves an external LED as shown above. You can read about \href{https://learn.sparkfun.com/tutorials/light-emitting-diodes-leds/}{\linkhilite{LEDs}} more online if you wish\hyperlink{sparkfun}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 59}]}}. \alert{Remember that the long leg of the LED is the positive end and the short leg is the negative end.} The task today is to wire an LED up to the CPX in multiple ways. The figure above shows the LED connected to the A1 pin but we will be wiring up the LED to multiple pins on the CPX\slash{}CPB to gain understanding in how the circuit works. \alert{Note, whenever you modify a circuit on the breadboard, always be sure to remove power from the CPX. You can damage multiple components if you're not careful.}%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.1 LED with no Code}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{LED with no Code}{}{LED with no Code}{}{}{led-buttons-3}
For this part we are going to light up the LED without the use of any code on the CPX. First, wire up the circuit with the positive end connected to 5V. This is how my circuit looks. Make sure to use a resistor between 300 and 1000 Ohms. An LED does not have that much internal resistance so you need a resistor in series with an LED to reduce the amount of current flowing through the LED or the entire LED will fry. If you use a resistor that has too much impedance the LED just won't turn on because the voltage\slash{}current through the LED will be below the activation voltage of the circuit.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express connected to an LED on a breadboard}{led-buttons-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LED1.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once you have that circuit working, wire up the circuit again with the 3.3V output. Do you notice anything different when you hook up the circuit with different pins? Here's my circuit. Do you notice something different about the intensity of the LED? Why is it different?%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express connected to an LED using 3.3V}{led-buttons-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LED2.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.2 LED with a push button}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{LED with a push button}{}{LED with a push button}{}{}{led-buttons-4}
Now that we understand breadboards a bit, we're now going to manually blink the LED using a push button placed onto the breadboard and have it act like a switch. Therefore, when the button is pressed, the LED will turn on and when the button is released the LED will turn off. The button just acts like a wire so you can plug in the button anywhere in the circuit.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express turning on an LED with an external push button}{led-buttons-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LED3.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.3 LED with code}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{LED with code}{}{LED with code}{}{}{led-buttons-5}
Next I want you to remove the button from the circuit and wire up the LED like you had it when the positive end was connected to VOUT or 3.3V. Except this time I want you to hook the positive end of the circuit to pin A2. Then edit your blink code to blink pin A2. Take a look at the blink code. Right now the code is blinking pin D13. How do you think you need to change the code to blink pin A2? Here's what my circuit looks like for this one. I won't include code for this one since you just need to change one line of code.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express connected to an LED with an analog pin}{led-buttons-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LED4.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.4 LED with CPX button}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{LED with CPX button}{}{LED with CPX button}{}{}{led-buttons-6}
Finally, I want to use one of the buttons on the CPX to blink the LED hooked up to pin A2. For this code to work you first need to detect a button press and then tell the program to change the light from True to False depending on what it's current status is. This one is a bit more difficult so I'll include the code here and discuss the code itself. Here is my circuit (identical) to the previous one with Button A on the CPX pressed down.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express powering an LED with an on-board button}{led-buttons-6-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LED5.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Alright so how do we detect a button press? Well the documentation on this is not so straight forward. What we want to do is detect the INPUT of a digital signal and then do something if we detect that signal. Here's the code I created to get it to work.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython code to power an LED with an on-board button}{led-buttons-6-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/button_blink.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code above is an image. You could type exactly what you see and hope you don't type any errors (which is highly unlikely) or you could look for my code on my \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/tree/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. Have you bookmarked this link yet? I recommend you do so!!! So you're making a code about Buttons....hmmm. Click that Github link. Where do you think the code about Buttons is located? Anywho, let's talk about the code.%
\par
The first 3 lines are exactly the same as before. Line 5 through 7 are similar to creating the \textquotedblleft{}led\textquotedblright{} variable except we're using \emph{BUTTON\textunderscore{}A} as the board value and setting the direction to \emph{INPUT}. Finally we're setting the pull direction to \emph{DOWN}. This means the button acts like a pull down resistor and when it's pressed the value of the button goes \emph{HIGH}.%
\par
Lines 9-11 are the same as before. We create an led variable and tell the CPX that the led is hooked up to pin A2. We then start the while loop on line 13. First if you click the Serial button you'll see the text \emph{Button value is False}. It's False because \emph{buttonA.value} is not being pressed. On line 15 the led is set to False (turned off). On line 16 the button value is checked using an if statement as to whether or not the button is pressed (True). If the button is pressed, the user will be notified that the button is pressed on line 17 and the led will be set to True (on) in line 18. Lines 19-22 are while loop that will notify the Serial monitor that you must let go of the button before the code can continue to the main while loop. The \emph{time.sleep} functions are there to make sure a human can operate the button without code running faster than a human can press a button. When I press the button down here is the output I get from the \emph{Serial} monitor.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython code to power an LED with an on-board button in Mu}{led-buttons-6-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/MuButton.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Here you'll see 4 lines that say \textquotedblleft{}Button value is False\textquotedblright{} and then two lines that say \textquotedblleft{}Button value is True\textquotedblright{} followed by 5 lines that say \textquotedblleft{}Waiting for you to let go...\textquotedblright{}. See if you can get this code to work and play with it and modify it as you see fit. By the way, the LED connected to pin D13 has this exact same circuitry, an LED a resistor, it's all just soldered to the PCB so you don't have to build it using a breadboard. Hopefully now you have some appreciation for buttons and LEDs!!%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.5 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{led-buttons-7}
If you're not familiar with how breadboards work I would recommend watching this \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mE33WpRWrXs}{\linkhilite{video on how breadboards work}}\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 8.6 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{led-buttons-8}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this specific project you must get your LED to turn on and off using buttons as well as software. The different scenarios are shown below and the grading rubric is presented after this list.%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Get your LED to turn on with the VOUT pin.%
\item{}Get your LED to turn on with the 3.3V pin.%
\item{}Get your LED to blink automatically using an analog pin on the CPX\slash{}CPB.%
\item{}Get your LED to turn on using a button on the breadboard.%
\item{}Get your LED to blink automatically by pressing a button on the CPX\slash{}CPB.%
\end{enumerate}
%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction with wiring diagram of LED connected to VOUT - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your LED turned on using VOUT. Based on the forward voltage in your LED and the resistance in your circuit, what is the voltage drop across the resistor and current through the LED? Assuming your LED acted like a resistor, what would the resistance of your LED be? - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your LED turned on using 3.3V. Based on the forward voltage in your LED and the resistance in your circuit, what is the voltage drop across the resistor and current through the LED? Assuming your LED acted like a resistor, what would the resistance of your LED be? - 10\%%
\item{}The USB port from your computer is 5V. The DC to DC converter on the CPX\slash{}CPB converts that 5V to 3.3V. Compute the Gain (\(G\)) for this converter both in magnitude and in \(dB\). - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your LED turned on using a button on your breadboard - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your LED turned on using an analog pin and then also include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of your Mu code showing the same analog pin used to blink your LED - 20\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your LED turned on by pressing a button on the CPX\slash{}CPB and then also include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of your Mu code showing the line of code used to read the button press and blink the LED. - 20\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen with the Serial Monitor open in Mu while you press and hold a button on the CPX\slash{}CPB and show the LED on your breadboard blink while you hold the button down%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 9 Logging Data}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Logging Data}{}{Logging Data}{}{}{daq}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{daq-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB%
\item{}USB Cable%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{daq-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Record real time measurements from the CircuitPlayground using the Serial monitor%
\item{}Learn how to use the typing module to type data directly into a spreadsheet or text file%
\item{}Learn how to log data on the on board memory of the CircuitPlayground%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
Taking data is the core of any instrumentation project. Data Acquisition Systems (DAQ) come in all shapes and sizes. Believe it or not the CPX can be used as a crude and cheap DAQ. The CPX can easily take temperature data and monitor the temperature in a greenhouse or take humidity readings of a plant to monitor soil content. Before we learn about the different sensors on board the CPX, we want to make sure we can store that data later rather than just having it spit data out via the serial monitor. In this chapter you're going to make a simple script that prints button presses and then log that in multiple different ways. The button presses are just a simple example but the same methods can be used to log any data you want from the CPX.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.1 Initial Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Initial Setup}{}{Initial Setup}{}{}{start}
For starters, let's get the CPX to print button presses. The code I'm using is shown below and can also be found on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Data_Logging/button_presses.py}{\linkhilite{Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython code that prints button presses}{start-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/button_presses.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code is pretty similar to what I had in the past. I import board, digitalio, and time. I create a buttonA object using the digitalio library to record button presses. I then enter into a while loop print the buttonA.value. The difference here is that I use the int() function to convert the buttonA.value to an integer. The reason why I do this is because buttonA.value is a boolean. It is either True or False. An integer though is a number and thus a value of False is 0 and True is 1. If you open the serial monitor and push the A button down a few times you'll see some zeros and 1's.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial monitor showing button presses in Mu}{start-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/MuButtonPress.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Mu also has a really neat builtin plotter. You'll see next to the Serial button there is a button called Plotter. If you click that button now nothing will pop up on the screen. Unfortunately in order to plot using the Plotter you need to modify the print() statement to this:%
\begin{preformatted}
print((int(buttonA.value),))
\end{preformatted}
Notice the extra parentheses and the comma. Now if you click Plotter you'll see something like this. You'll notice that the print statement now has commas in it and the Plotter is recording button presses.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Plotter open showing button presses in Mu}{start-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
