\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/MuPlotter.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The problem with this is we still can't save the recorded data anywhere. Before we get into saving data let's first edit the print statement again to get rid of the Plotter by removing the extra parentheses and add time.monotonic() that way we can keep track of when a button was pressed. My print statement looks like this now:%
\begin{preformatted}
print(time.monotonic(),int(buttonA.value))
\end{preformatted}
Looking at the serial monitor now you'll see that time is being printed alongside the button presses.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial monitor showing button presses and time in Mu}{start-13}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/MuPlotterTime.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Now we are in a position where we can record some data and save it to our computer. There are 4 ways to record data. I call the first, Method1 and you basically just copy and paste from the serial monitor, Method2 where you have the CPX\slash{}CPB type data into a spreadsheet and Method3 where you log data internally onto the CPX\slash{}CPB itself. The 4th method called Method4 utilizes the Bluetooth Module. Since that has it's own issues there is a completely separate section on how to explain Bluetooth (See \hyperref[bluetooth]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{bluetooth}}}}). Note you can only do Bluetooth if you have the Circuit Playground Bluefruit (CPB).%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.2 Method 1 - Copying Serial Monitor Data}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 1 - Copying Serial Monitor Data}{}{Method 1 - Copying Serial Monitor Data}{}{}{daq-4}
If you open up the serial monitor you can see the data output. If you unplug the CPX while it's taking data the code will stop. \alert{Note: In newer versions, unplugging your CPX will result in a loss of data. If this happens try pressing CTRL+C after you click the REPL window.} With the code stopped you can select all the data in the Serial monitor and then copy and paste the data into a text file on your computer. You can actually copy this into a new file on Thonny and just save it as a \textasteriskcentered{}.txt file. Once you have the data in a text file you can proceed to plotting on your desktop which I discuss in the last section. Here's some example data in Gedit which is a simple text editing program.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example button data in a text editor}{daq-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Gedit_Data.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.3 Method 2 - Automatically Populate a Spreadsheet}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 2 - Automatically Populate a Spreadsheet}{}{Method 2 - Automatically Populate a Spreadsheet}{}{}{daq-5}
The downside with the above method of course is if you have a ton of data to record you could lose the data or run into a massive copy and paste issue. The second option is to use this module called keyboard which takes control of your keyboard on your desktop computer and actively types your data into a spreadsheet. The code is very extensive but I'll include the simple one here so we can discuss it. Below are the first 30 lines of code. The first 6 lines of code are just comments since I heavily adopted this code from the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/make-it-a-keyboard/circuitpython}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Learn System}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Data_Logging/typing/record_button_presses_typing.py}{\linkhilite{My version of this code}} can be found on my Github \hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. Lines 8 - 14 are import commands as we've seen previously. The regular import modules board, time and digitalio are imported but we are also importing the Keyboard module so that the CPX can takeover our keyboard. In order to import that module properly you need to \href{https://circuitpython.org}{\linkhilite{install modules}} onto you \emph{lib} folder (See \hyperref[troubleshooting]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{troubleshooting}}}}) \hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Lines 16-22 create two buttons. First we create buttonA attached to pin D4 and then a switch attached to pin D7. If you look on the CPX there is a switch labeled D7. Before you copy this code onto the CPX make sure you move the switch towards the ear looking symbol. Lines 26-28 created the keyboard object. We are going to call it layout for this example code.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Snippet of Typing code in CircuitPython}{daq-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Typing1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The next 30 lines are shown below. Lines 32-35 define a function. Functions in CircuitPython have a pretty standard structure. The keyword def is used to denote that the next line is a definition for a function. The name of the function is \emph{slow\textunderscore{}write()}. The input to the function is string which ironically enough is a string object. Line 33-35 define what the function does. Line 33 sets up a for loop where the code loops through each character in the string. Everytime it gets to a new character it will use your keyboard to type that character using the layout.write(c) command. The time.sleep(0.02) is just to slow down the keyboard so your computer can keep up. That function is defined above the standard while True: statement on line 37 but is called on line 42. You'll see there is a \emph{slow\textunderscore{}write(output)} on line 42. In this case output is a string and it's sent to the function \emph{slow\textunderscore{}write()}. So in this case we have a function that can write a string so we just need to take data and then write it using our keyboard. Line 38 is an if statement that will only be true if the switch on pin D7 is pushed towards the music note on the CPX. If the switch is not thrown the code will move to the else statement on line 52 and tell the user that you need to flip the switch. If the switch is thrown line 40 will take data for us. First it will record the time.monotonic() and store it as a floating point number using the \%0.1f designation which means that it will store 1 decimal as a \%floating point number for f.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Remainder of Typing code in CircuitPython}{daq-5-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Typing2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The second number in the string is an integer or a base 10 (decimal) integer designated by the \%d part of the format. The integer is int(buttonA.value). You'll see a \textbackslash{}t in between the formatted numbers which is a tab. The tab is there to tab between cells in a spreadsheet. Line 41 will print the output string to the Serial monitor and it will also type the contents of the string. Very important here. When you flip the switch on the CPX your keyboard will start typing in whatever active window is selected. If you don't have a spreadsheet opened and active (selected), the keyboard will just begin typing in whatever window is open. Make sure you have a spreadsheet program open and ready to go. Lines 44-51 tell the keyboard to hit the \emph{DOWN\textunderscore{}ARROW} on your keyboard to move to the next row and the \emph{LEFT\textunderscore{}ARROW} twice to move back to the first column. Line 55 is a sleep to only log data once a second. I ran this code for a bit and had it type into LibreOffice Calc which is a free spreadsheet program. Google Sheets or Microsoft Excel will also work just fine.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example data in a spreadsheet from Typing code}{daq-5-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Typing3.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
You'll see that the first column is time with 1 decimal point and the second column is the button press values. At this point you must click \emph{Save As...} and save the document as a CSV which stands for Comma Separated Value. Once you have the file saved you can proceed to plotting on your Desktop.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.4 Method 3 - Logging Data Directly to on board memory}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 3 - Logging Data Directly to on board memory}{}{Method 3 - Logging Data Directly to on board memory}{}{}{daq-6}
The problem with the above 2 methods is that you need a laptop to log data in the field. It would be nice if you could use the optional battery pack and just have the CPX log data on the CPX itself. This is the most complex way but in my opinion the best way. In order to get this to work you need to allow the drive on the CPX to have read\slash{}write permissions. This requires you to load a piece of software called boot.py and put it on the CPX. I have this \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Data_Logging/boot.py}{\linkhilite{software on my Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. The software is shown below. The first 10 lines are probably very familiar. Import some modules and then create a switch object. Line 13 is where all of the storage permissions are changed. If the flip is switched towards the A button, the storage module is used to allow you to write to the CPX. The problem here is that if you do this, you won't be able to edit code. I'll explain the procedure here in a minute. As always, the relevant Adafruit tutorial is on the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-express/circuitpython-storage}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Learn System}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}} if you want to read more about it. Again make sure you store this file onto the CIRCUITPY drive and save it as boot.py%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{boot.py script in CircuitPython}{daq-6-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/boot.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In addition to storing the file boot.py you'll need to edit your main.py script to only log data when the switch is moved towards the B button. The software to record button presses on disk is shown below and as always \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Data_Logging/write_button_presses_disk.py}{\linkhilite{on my Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. In this software we again see the standard commands. Lines 1-3 import all the modules we need and then 5-15 create a switch, a button and an LED. In this case we're using the LED soldered to the board. Line 17-20 check to see if the user has flipped the switch. If the switch is False the storage module on boot.py will allow the drive to act like a data logger and it will open a file called \emph{Test\textunderscore{}Data.txt} for writing \emph{(\textquotedblleft{}w\textquotedblright{})}. If the switch is True then the user will be notified that the file has not been opened for writing. Lines 22 through 33 include the infinite while loop. Line 23 turns the LED on and line 24 prints out the current time and the button value in integer form. If the switch value is False the program will create an output string by converting all numbers to strings using the str function. Notice that there is a \emph{str(\textquotedblleft{}\textbackslash{}textbackslash n\textquotedblright{})} at the end of the output variable which tells the computer to write a new line of data to the file. Lines 28 and 29 write the output to the file from line 18 and then flush the output which means the CPX waits for the data to be fully written before moving on. It also turns the LED off so we know the CPX took data even when we aren't looking at the Serial monitor. If the switch value is true it means that the we never opened the data file and thus we tell the user we aren't logging data and it's time to flip the switch and hit reset. \alert{NOTE: The figure below is from an old version of the code. The newest version produces the same outcome. The only difference is that the D13 LED blinks when the code is running and the first neopixel toggles 3 different colors when the system is logging data. This allows for extra user information when operating Method 3 without a computer and the serial monitor. Note that these additions require the use of neopixel.mpy module.}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Snippet of writing data to a text file in CircuitPython}{daq-6-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/method3_1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
So here is the flow of what you want to do for method 3.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Unplug the CPX%
\item{}Flip the switch towards the A button.%
\item{}Plug in the CPX and save the boot.py and main.py files. Remember you can only save CircuitPython scripts when the switch is flipped towards the A button.%
\item{}When you are ready to start recording data, flip the switch towards the B button. If you're looking at the Serial monitor, the software will throw an error. Just ignore it and hit the reset button. When your computer recognizes the CPX you can turn the Serial monitor on and off.%
\item{}When you are done taking data simply slide the switch over towards the A button and hit reset again. This is what my Serial monitor looks like when I do this. You'll see that I was writing to disk for like 25 seconds and then I flipped the switch back towards the A button.%
\end{enumerate}
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial monitor open in Mu showing Method 3 for data logging}{daq-6-8}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/method3_2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
With the switch flipped and data taken, open your folder manager and take a look at the CIRCUITPY drive. This is what mine looks like. You'll see I have two CircuitPython files and a file \emph{Test\textunderscore{}Data.txt} with all my data in it.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Text file shown in file manager on computer}{daq-6-10}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/method3_3.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
If you open the \emph{Test\textunderscore{}Data.txt} file you will hopefully see data in it.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example text file from Method 3}{daq-6-12}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Gedit_Data.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
At this point you can copy this text file over to your desktop computer and proceed to the plotting portion. Ok so let's recap method 3 one more time.%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Unplug CPX (or remove power)%
\item{}Slide switch to A%
\item{}Plug in CPX (or provide battery power) and wait for system to fully boot up%
\item{}Slide switch to B%
\item{}Hit Reset and wait for system to fully boot up%
\item{}Take data for however long you want%
\item{}Slide switch to A when you're ready to stop taking data%
\item{}Hit Reset and wait for system to fully boot up%
\item{}Remove power if you're on battery power%
\item{}Plug CPX into computer if not already connected%
\item{}Transfer data file to computer%
\end{enumerate}
%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.5 Method 4 - Logging Data on a Cell Phone using Bluetooth (CPB Only)}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 4 - Logging Data on a Cell Phone using Bluetooth (CPB Only)}{}{Method 4 - Logging Data on a Cell Phone using Bluetooth (CPB Only)}{}{}{daq-7}
As mentioned in the introduction it's possible to have the Circuit Playground send data wirelessly to a cell phone using Bluetooth provided you have Bluetooth setup and a smart phone with the Adafruit Connect App. Bluetooth is explained in detail in its own chapter (See \hyperref[bluetooth]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{bluetooth}}}}). Method 4 is a valid form of logging data it just requires a cell phone to be powered the entire time and it must be within 30 feet of the Circuit Playground at all times. This also only works on the CPB since the CPX does not have a Bluetooth transmitter.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.6 Method 5 - Logging Data to an SD Card}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 5 - Logging Data to an SD Card}{}{Method 5 - Logging Data to an SD Card}{}{}{daq-8}
Another to log data is using a standalone \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/254}{\linkhilite{micro sd card breakout board}} (See \hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}})\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. Method 1 and 2 are simple to use but they require a laptop and Method 4 can only be done with a CPB. Method 3 is light-weight in the sense that you don't need any extra components but this method can be somewhat unreliable. It's possible to brick the CPX which requires formatting (See \hyperref[troubleshooting]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{troubleshooting}}}}) or data corruption in general to the file. I've seen so many students not follow Method 3 correctly and either not get data or corrupt their entire CPX which requires them to reformat. Although some students have been successful, the complexity of getting \emph{boot.py} to work properly requires great care and this is where Method 5 excels. In this method rather than logging data directly to the CPX\slash{}CPB and potentially corrupting the internal memory, the data is logged to an external SD card. After logging data the card can be removed and uploaded to a computer using any SD card to USB converter.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.7 Method 6 - Logging Data via Serial}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Method 6 - Logging Data via Serial}{}{Method 6 - Logging Data via Serial}{}{}{daq-9}
Yet another method to log data is using the same serial communication that is used in Mu (See \hyperref[serial]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{serial}}}}). In this case you need your laptop just like method 1 and 2 but you forgo having to copy and paste the Serial monitor yourself. In this method you would have a program on your computer that automatically reads the serial output from the CPX and saves that to a text file. It reduces the complexity of the CircuitPython code and allows you to focus on the experiment while your computer handles logging data.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.8 Plotting Logged Data}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Plotting Logged Data}{}{Plotting Logged Data}{}{}{daq-10}
Alright so there you have it. I have explained 4 methods to datalogging. Here are the methods again in summary.%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Print data to Serial and copy and paste%
\item{}Use the Keyboard module to save data to a spreadsheet%
\item{}Access the storage of your CPX and write data to a text file on the CPX%
\item{}Send data wirelessly to a Cell Phone using Bluetooth - CPB Only (See \hyperref[bluetooth]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{bluetooth}}}})%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
All methods will work but some will obviously have their pros and cons. I suggest you get comfortable with 1 method and use that for the remainder of the semester. Whatever option you choose though will provide you with a data file that you can open on your desktop computer to plot. The simplest way to import data is by using the loadtxt function from the module numpy. Here is some very simple code to plot data from a text file.%
\par
When you plot make sure your \emph{Test\textunderscore{}Data.txt} file is in the same folder as your plotting script in Thonny or Spyder. Here's my example code (this code is not on Github but you only need 3 or 4 lines of code to plot).%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Plotted data shown in Thonny}{daq-10-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/plotdata.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this example lines 1 and 2 import numpy and matplotlib. Line 4 imports data from the \emph{Test\textunderscore{}Data.txt} file and then 6 and 7 save the first and second columns into time and button. The remaining lines plot the data and create x and y labels as well as a grid. Hopefully now you are well versed in taking data and plotting on your desktop computer.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.9 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{daq-11}
This is a pretty hard project to do with multiple different methods. After you've read through this document I suggest you watch a youtube I created on \href{https://youtu.be/yX0zeIfn2j4}{\linkhilite{Logging Data with the Circuit Playground Express}}. I have also made video where I \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Vc7_KxRnORI}{\linkhilite{log temperature and accelerometer data using on board memory with the CircuitPlayground Bluefruit}}. Finally, I also have a \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tJOz-ty-2ec\&list=PL_D7_GvGz-v1RsDs_OdNW65qRjEjmpfQx\&index=12}{\linkhilite{Youtube video explaining how to plot a text file}}\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 9.10 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{daq-12}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this project you must use method 1, 2, 3 or 4 to save time and button presses to a text file. You must then plot the button presses as a function of time. Remember to add x and y labels to all figures.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a snippet (5 lines) of your data file - 40\%%
\item{}Include a plot of your button presses with time on the x-axis and button presses on the y-axis (no screenshots) - 40\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - File yourself and your entire screen pressing the button and showing the number change on the screen, then show yourself plotting your data on your computer - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 10 Bluetooth Logging - Method 4}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Bluetooth Logging - Method 4}{}{Bluetooth Logging - Method 4}{}{}{bluetooth}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{bluetooth-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Smart Phone%
\item{}Adafruit BLE Connect App (\href{https://play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.adafruit.bluefruit.le.connect}{\linkhilite{Play Store}}\slash{}\href{https://apps.apple.com/us/app/bluefruit-connect/id830125974}{\linkhilite{App Store}})\hyperlink{AdafruitBLE}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 36}]}}%
\item{}CircuitPlayground Bluefruit%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Laptop%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{bluetooth-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Understand the bluetooth module on the CircuitPlayground Bluefruit%
\item{}Learn how to send data via the Bluefruit to your smart phone%
\item{}Understand how to plot data sent via UART%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
I mentioned in the DAQ project that Method 4 can be used to log data by using bluetooth to send data from your phone to your Circuitplayground Bluefruit (CPB) and vice versa. Once the data is on your phone you can export the data to a text file. That basically means you can use the bluetooth module as another method to save data from the CPB. There is a lot you can do with bluetooth but for starters all code required for this module is on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/tree/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/CPB_DataLogger}{\linkhilite{Github}}. This chapter walks through how to write some example bluetooth code and get the app to receive the data on your smart phone.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 10.1 Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setup}{}{Setup}{}{}{bluetooth-3}
First we're going to run the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/CPB_DataLogger/bluefruit_uart_button.py}{\linkhilite{bluetooth\textunderscore{}uart\textunderscore{}button.py}} script which sends button data to your smart phone via something called UART which is a type of serial communication. It's beyond the scope of this lesson but serial is digital as opposed to analog which is done using the AnalogIn functions (See \hyperref[voltage]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{voltage}}}}) \hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Bluetooth code in CircuitPython}{bluetooth-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/bluetooth_code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Lines 3-11 import a ton of modules. You'll recognize many of them like analogio, and time but the new ones are the ones that say ble. These are the bluetooth modules required for the CPB. Lines 21-24 setup the button so we can log the button via bluetooth and Lines 14-17 kick of the BLERadio object and the UARTService() to send data. Line 25 also grabs the current time before the infinite while loop that way the timer starts closer to zero.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Remainder of Bluetooth code in CircuitPython}{bluetooth-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/bluetooth_code1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code above is the infinite while loop which actually contains 2 while loops. Lines 28-33 prints the name of your bluetooth sensor and then starts advertising bluetooth to whoever is listening. It will then enter a while loop from 32-33 until bluetooth is connected. Once bluetooth is connected it will enter into the second while loop from line 39-52. In those lines 40-46 is responsible for taking all the necessary measurements and printing them to the serial monitor in Mu. In this case it's only printing the current time and the value of the button as an integer. \emph{buttonA.value} is either True or False and the \emph{int} function converts that to a 0 or a 1. Line 49 then sends the data over bluetooth using the UART server. You'll notice in this case the code is sending t,b by using the format variable and the 2 empty  brackets. If you want to send more data you need to add more empty brackets and more variables to the format function. When you first save this script your CPB will not be connected and enter into an infinite while loop where it waits for your smart phone to connect. If you open your smart phone and open the Bluefruit Connect App the following screen will pop up.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Screenshot of Bluetooth devices on a smart phone}{bluetooth-3-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.35}{0.3}{0.35}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/phoneapp1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case there are numerous different bluetooth modules can be seen but the one you need to click is the one that says CIRCUITPYf8e8. You will have a different code after CIRCUITPY and you can figure out what your 4 digit code is by making sure you have the \emph{print('Look for',ble.name)} in your code. Once you do that the CPB will begin sending time and the button press to your smart phone.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Interfaces in Bluetooth App on smart phone}{bluetooth-3-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.35}{0.3}{0.35}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/phoneapp2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
There are numerous items you can click. The Controller is very fun for creating a remote control robot but we're only going to go over the UART and Plotter tabs. If you click the plotter tab you will be greeted with a live screen of the data being sent.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example plotter in Bluetooth App on smart phone}{bluetooth-3-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.35}{0.3}{0.35}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/phoneapp3.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the photo above you can see three data streams that is coming directly from the CPB. The red line is time and the blue line is the button value. Notice the blue line goes from 0 to 1 which means I pressed the button a few times. The red line is always increasing which kind of messes up the plotter so you can always go back to your code in Mu and just send your data. This is great for live demonstrations and for debugging if you need to see data from an experiment and you don't have access to a laptop with Mu. If you hit the back arrow and then click UART you will see the raw data come in as text.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example UART data in Bluetooth App on smart phone}{bluetooth-3-13}{}%
\begin{image}{0.35}{0.3}{0.35}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/phoneapp4.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Again here you can see the 3 data streams separated by commas. The very neat thing with the UART tab is that you can click the three vertical dots in the upper right hand corner and click export to TXT. The easiest thing for me was to export the data to google drive and then download the data to my computer. Once I downloaded the TXT file to my computer and opened it the data file looked like this.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example data exported to CSV file}{bluetooth-3-15}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/csv_fileapp.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
If you export the file as a CSV the data file will look completely different and it's much more complicated to plot. If you export the data as a TXT file you just need to use the np.loadtxt command to read in the data. Note you might have commas in your data file. If there are commas just use the CTRL+H command and replace all commas with spaces or use the np.loadtxt('buttonble.txt',delimiter=',') command. Plotting your button presses should be as simple as the previous lab thus plotting the button is left as an exercise to the reader.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 10.2 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{bluetooth-4}
You might find plotting data via bluetooth to be rather difficult and it was pretty difficult for me until I learned that you can export data as a txt file rather than a csv file. Before I learned how to do that I put together a \href{https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PL_D7_GvGz-v0-U3JACRMgldvqQqn2mje9}{\linkhilite{4 part series}} describing everything in this module. Worst case you can just watch the \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9EHNFdVX9O8\&list=PL_D7_GvGz-v0-U3JACRMgldvqQqn2mje9\&index=3}{\linkhilite{third video in the series}}. The video is 30 minutes but the first 8 minutes goes through setting up the bluetooth module and the rest of the video is just on plotting the exported csv data which took me some time. Note that exporting data as a txt file is the preferred method as parsing the file is way easier \hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 10.3 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{bluetooth-5}
\begin{introduction}{}%
This project is similar to the Data Acquisition project only you must use method 4 to save time and button presses to a text file. You must then plot the button presses as a function of time. Remember to add x and y labels to all figures.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire screen) from your phone showing the raw UART output - 20\%%
\item{}Include a snippet (5 lines) of your data file - 20\%%
\item{}Include a plot of your button presses with time on the x-axis and button presses on the y-axis (no screenshots) - 40\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing data broadcast to your phone while you press and release the button, then show yourself plotting the data on your computer - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 11 SD Card Logging - Method 5}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{SD Card Logging - Method 5}{}{SD Card Logging - Method 5}{}{}{sdcard}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{sdcard-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Micro SD Card%
\item{}Micro SD Card USB Reader%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/254}{\linkhilite{Micro SD Card Breakout Board}}%
\item{}Arduino or CPX\slash{}CPB%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{sdcard-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Understand how to use a micro SD card to log data from a microcontroller%
\item{}Learn to install libraries for the Arduino%
\item{}Learn yet another way to use a microcontroller as a data acquisition system%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
This chapter explores writing data directly to a micro SD card. This is helpful because when the datalogger is done taking data, it is easy to power down the system, take the SD card out and pop it into a laptop for post-processing. For the CPX\slash{}CPB setp it helps because you don't have to have data writing to the internal storage which can potentially brick the system. You have code and data storage in two different places. Setting up this system requires a few steps for both CPX\slash{}CPB and Arduino microcontrollers. The Arduino UNO is capable of writing to the micro SD card but the only difference is that the pins setup is different. There is a very indepth \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-micro-sd-breakout-board-card-tutorial}{\linkhilite{SD card tutorial on Adafruit}} that can be used in conjunction with this tutorial if extra help is needed \hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Micro SD card Breakout Board without header pins soldered}{sdcard-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/microSDcard.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
No matter what for both microcontrollers you need to have a micro SD card formatted to FAT32. In Linux that means you need to navigate to the Disk utility app and format the disk. On Windows or Mac you need to use a similar program to format the disk.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Using the Disk Utility to Format a Micro SD Card on Linux}{sdcard-2-6}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/SDCARD_FORMAT.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Also, when you open the packaging of this breakout board you'll notice that the header pins are missing. First you'll need to cut a row of 8 to \alert{solder the header pins to the board}. If you're taking my class I can solder this for you or teach everyone about soldering during a lecture session of class. If you are taking this class elsewhere you have two options: try and find someone who can solder this real quick (only takes about 5 minutes) or buy your own soldering iron and try to solder yourself. Once the device is soldered you can "plug" it into a breadboard and then move on to either the Arduino or CPX\slash{}CPB sections.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 11.1 Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{Setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB}{}{}{sdcard-3}
Before wiring up the system I recommend setting up the CPX\slash{}CPB first before wiring up the system. The example code we're going to use for this chapter is on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/SD/sdcard_cpb_button_presses.py}{\linkhilite{Github}} as usual.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Snippet of SD card Software for the CPB\slash{}CPX}{sdcard-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/SDcard_Snippet_CPB.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The figure above shows the preamble of the code which has a few things that you need to do before getting this to work. \alert{First, you need to have an \emph{sd} folder in the your \emph{CIRCUITPY} drive.} That means, when you plug in the CPB\slash{}CPX to your computer you must add a solder called \emph{sd} into the main folder alongside the \emph{lib} folder which needs the \emph{adafruit\textunderscore{}sdcard} module. Before installing extra modules into the \emph{lib} folder you want to make sure you have your \href{https://circuitpython.org/downloads}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python UF2}} up to date\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. In this example I'm using the 10.X version. Once I updated my UF2 I also updated my \href{https://circuitpython.org/libraries}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python Libraries}} (See \hyperref[installing-modules]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{installing-modules}}}} for help on installing extra modules on your CPX\slash{}CPB)\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Again, for this lab you just need the \emph{adafruit\textunderscore{}sdcard} module in the lib folder. After that you can copy the software from Github into your \emph{code.py} file and save it. The code will not run right away and will probably through an \emph{SD Card Not Found} error. At this point you can unplug the CPX\slash{}CPB and begin wiring the breakout board making to plug in the micro SD card of course.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CPB wired to a micro SD card breakout board}{sdcard-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CPB_Method5_Wiring.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the figure above moving counter-clockwise, the red alligator clip from \emph{5V} to a green male-male wire to \emph{5V}, the first yellow alligator clip goes directly from \emph{A1(CLK)} to \emph{CLK}, the first green alligator clip goes from \emph{A2(MISO)} to a white male-male wire to \emph{DO}, the second green alligator clip goes from \emph{A3(MOSI)} to a blue male-male wire to \emph{DI}, the second yellow alligator clip is connected to \emph{A4} then to a black male-male wire connected to \emph{CS} and finally the black alligator clip goes directly from \emph{GND} to \emph{GND}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example output when writing to the SD card}{sdcard-3-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CPB_Method5_DatFileOutput.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once you plug in the CPB\slash{}CPX and press the A button to start recording you can press the A button over and over again to stop logging and then restart logging. Everytime you close the file and open a new file, the code will find a new file to create so that you don't continue to overwrite the file that is already on the sd card. In this code the A button is used to start and stop recording and the B button along with a dummy counter is logged to the SD card.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Data file after writing to SD Card}{sdcard-3-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/DataFile_CPB_Data.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
When you are done recording make sure to press the A button to the close the current file and then remove power from the CPX\slash{}CPB. You can then place the SD card into your computer and you should see multiple \emph{DataFile\#.txt} text files in your computer. You can then copy those to your computer and plot the data similar to \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}. This code can easily be augmented to accommodate any other sensor such as GPS (\hyperref[gps]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{gps}}}}), accelerometer (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}), pressure (\hyperref[pressure]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{pressure}}}}) or any other sensor you can think of. Although you add the complexity of having an extra piece of hardware, you remove the software headache and potential bricking of your CPX\slash{}CPB by attempting to use Method 3 (\hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}) while still removing the need for a laptop.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 11.2 Setting up the Arduino}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setting up the Arduino}{}{Setting up the Arduino}{}{}{sdcard-4}
There is no setup for the Arduino other than making sure the SD card is formatted to \emph{FAT32} and that the breakout board is soldered properly to header pins. The wiring for an Arduino also requires 5 wires that I recommend doing while the system is powered off.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Micro SD card Breakout Board wired to an Arduino MEGA}{sdcard-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/MEGA_SD_2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the figure above the dark green male-male wire goes from \emph{5V} to \emph{5V}, the orange male-male wire goes from \emph{GND} to \emph{GND}, the light green wire goes from \emph{52} to \emph{CLK}, the white wire goes from \emph{50} to \emph{DO}, the purple male-male wire goes from \emph{51} to \emph{DI} and the blue male-male wire goes from \emph{53} to \emph{CS}. The software to get this to work is on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/SD_Example/SD_Example.ino}{\linkhilite{Github}} as usual and a snippet is shown in the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Software Snippet to send data to a micro SD card breakout board}{sdcard-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/SDCardSoftware_Arduino.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
You'll notice in this code that there is a \emph{\#include \textless{}SD.h\textgreater{}} line of code which imports the SD header file. In C++ and other compilation languages you have a \emph{.cpp} or \emph{.c} file which has all the code necessary to run somthing and a \emph{.h} file which declares all the variables. In Python you don't have to declare a variable the language figures it out on its own but in Arduino you must tell the computer what type of variable you are using. In this case if you need an extra module you don't import it you include it. To include extra module you must click \emph{Sketch\textgreater{}Include Library\textgreater{}Manage Libraries...}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Installing New Arduino Libraries}{sdcard-4-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_Libraries_1.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
After clicking that a new window will pop up where you can then search for "SD.h" and after scrolling down a bit you'll find the SD library to install. After installing it you will be able to Compile the software without any errors and Upload the script to your Arduino. The Serial monitor will work with this script as \emph{Serial.begin(115200)} is in the setup routine and there are many \emph{Serial.print} calls in the loop routine. If you open the serial monitor you should see the \emph{millis()} time printed on the screen provided you set the baud rate to 115200.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino Serial Monitor Output when writing to an SD Card}{sdcard-4-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/SD_Arduino_Serial.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case the serial monitor also states that is is writing the \emph{DATA004.TXT}. Everytime the code runs it will look for a new file that doesn't exist so that everytime you boot up the code it will create a new file. It ends up creating a lot of files but at least your data isn't overwritten. It also only outputs the internal timer because the Arduino does not have a built-in button but once we do other labs with pressure and acceleration it will be easy to send that data to the SD card as is shown in the code snippet below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Code to send acceleration data to an SD card}{sdcard-4-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Acceleration_SD_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code to get an external accelerometer to work on an Arduino is dicussed in more detail in \hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}. Still, for the simple example of just writing time to an SD card, by simply unplugging the Arduino and placing the SD card into an SD card reader will show all the files on your laptop and opening the txt file itself will show all the data you just took.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Data files on a micro SD Card}{sdcard-4-13}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/SD_Arduino_Data.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
At this point it is identical to \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}} to plot the data in whatever plotting environment you like.%
\end{sectionptx}

\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 12 Logging with Serial - Method 6}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Logging with Serial - Method 6}{}{Logging with Serial - Method 6}{}{}{serial}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{serial-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Computer with Python installed%
\item{}CircuitPlayground Express or Bluefruit%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{serial-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Understand how to use serial communication to log data from a microcontroller%
\item{}Learn to read and interpret serial data in Python%
\item{}Create a simple data logging script using the serial library%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
Method 6 is another "automatic" way to log data from your microcontroller. This method uses the serial library in Python to read data sent from your CircuitPlayground Express or Bluefruit. The idea is to have the Circuit Playground send data via USB to your laptop just like the REPL in Mu but instead of just viewing the output in Mu a Python script on a laptop can read and process the data. Serial communication is a fundamental method for microcontrollers to send data to a computer. In this chapter, we will explore how to use the serial library in Python to read data from your CircuitPlayground Express or Bluefruit. This method allows you to log data in real-time and can be a powerful tool for data acquisition and analysis. The code on the CPX\slash{}CPB is the same button press code used for the last few chapters and again can be found on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Data_Logging/button_presses.py}{\linkhilite{Github}}. The code itself is discussed in detail in Chapter \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 12.1 Serial Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Serial Setup}{}{Serial Setup}{}{}{serial-3}
In order to read serial data from a microcontroller, the serial library must be installed in Python. The serial library allows the computer to communicate with the serial port on a computer and read data sent from the microcontroller. The steps to install the serial module is the same as installing \emph{numpy} or \emph{matplotlib} explained in Chapter \hyperref[plotting]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{plotting}}}}. If using Spyder or command line the command is shown below.%
\begin{preformatted}
!pip install serial
\end{preformatted}
or%
\begin{preformatted}
!pip3 install serial
\end{preformatted}
if using Python3. If using Thonny, the process is the same as installing any other module by going to Tools \textgreater{} Manage Packages. The code to read serial data from the CPX\slash{}CPB is shown below and can also be found on \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Serial_Handshake/read_serial_cpb.py}{\linkhilite{Github}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Serial code in Python}{serial-3-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Serial_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code above is pretty advanced and uses a class called \emph{CPX} to handle all of the serial communication as well as saving the text file and plotting. The \emph{\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}init\textunderscore{}\textunderscore{}} function creates a filename and opens a serial port on "\slash{}dev\slash{}ttyACM0" which is a serial port for Linux. If using Windows the port will be something like "COM3" and if using Mac it will be something like "\slash{}dev\slash{}tty.usbmodem14101". The \emph{read} function reads the serial data and saves it to a text file by first stripping the binary information, line feeds and converts the number of a 64 bit number. The \emph{extractdata} function plots the data after the serial reading is done. The main part of the code is in the while loop at the bottom which continuously reads serial data for 10 seconds in this case. So basically, the CPX\slash{}CPB is coded using Mu to send serial data to the computer just like the REPL in Mu and then the laptop program is run to save 10 second of data to a text file and then plot the data. The figure below shows the result of running the example script. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example Serial Plot of Button Presses vs Time}{serial-3-8-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Plot_Serial.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
%
\end{sectionptx}

%

The first video discusses \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULAeE9UxtHc}{\linkhilite{how to use the "screen" program on Ubuntu (Linux) to read serial data from a microcontroller without the use of Mu}}. This is a great starting point to see how serial communication is sent to a laptop via USB. The second video is a bit more advanced and shows \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UG6aolK8p3I}{\linkhilite{how to use the serial library in Python to read serial data from a microcontroller}}. The video walks through the code explained in the previous sections and is a great starting point if video lectures are preferred to text%
\end{sectionptx}

\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Potentiometer}{}{Potentiometer}{}{}{voltage}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{voltage-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Potentiometer%
\item{}Resistor (the Ohms depends on how large your potentiometer is)%
\item{}Breadboard%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{voltage-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Understand voltage division of resistors in series%
\item{}Measure an analog signal on the CircuitPlayground%
\item{}Understand the binary measurement done by the analog to digital conversion (ADC)%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
At this point in a standard Instrumentation course, you've learned about analog to digital converters (ADC). It turns out that the CPX has 8 analog ports hooked up to a 3.3V logic 16 bit ADC. The input range on the ADC is 0 to 3.3V and the output range is 0 to 65536 which is \(2^{16}\) hence 16 bits. In order to get accustomed to the ADC on the CPX, we're going to do a simple example where we measure the voltage drop across a potentiometer. You can read about \href{https://www.build-electronic-circuits.com/potentiometer/}{\linkhilite{potentiometers online if you wish}}\hyperlink{potentiometerlearn}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 60}]}}. Basically though, a potentiometer is a variable resistance resistor that changes resistance by turning a knob. The knob changes the connection point of a wire and thus the length of the wire. This in turn changes the resistance. Potentiometers come in all shapes and sizes. Here are some \href{https://uk.rs-online.com/web/content/discovery/ideas-and-advice/potentiometers-guide}{\linkhilite{potentiometer examples}}. I've done this lab with a few potentiometer. Ideally you'd like to have the potentiometer hooked up in series with another resistor so that you end up building a voltage divider but it' possible you can do it without it as shown in the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/sensor-plotting-with-mu-and-circuitpython/potentiometer}{\linkhilite{figure below}} (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/kattni}{\linkhilite{Kattni Rembor}}) \hyperlink{potentiometer}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 38}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Wiring Diagram of a Circuit Playground Express hooked up to a Potentiometer\hyperlink{potentiometer}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 38}]}}}{voltage-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/sensors_PlottingMuPotentiometer_bb.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this chapter you're going to wire up a potentiometer to the CPX and read the voltage across it as you turn the knob.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 13.1 Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setup}{}{Setup}{}{}{voltage-3}
Here's my circuit all hooked up without a resistor in series. Two legs are connected to 3.3V and GND while the middle leg of the potentiometer is connected to pin A2.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Circuit Playground Express hooked up to a Potentiometer}{voltage-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/potentiometer1.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\alert{As I said before, some potentiometers do not have enough resistance when turned all the way down. I suggest that you put a resistor in between the third leg and ground. Some experimenters have melted plastic or gotten really hot. One student even blew up a potentiometer.} Here is my circuit with a resistor in series.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Potentiometer with a blue resistor in series}{voltage-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/potentiometer2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
There is a relevant \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-circuit-playground-express/circuitpython-analog-in}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Learn Tutorial}} to help with the \emph{analogio} module but I'll explain the minimum required here to get some analog values plotted in \emph{Plotter} and on your computer\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. First let's take a look at some \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Analog/analog_simple.py}{\linkhilite{simple example code}} to read an analog signal and plot it using the \emph{Plotter}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Code to read analog signal in CircuitPython}{voltage-3-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.25}{0.5}{0.25}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/analogio.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the example code above, lines 1-3 again import the necessary modules with \emph{analogio} being the new module here. Line 5 creates the analog object by attaching pin A2 to the analog function. Lines 7-9 then simple read the analog value and print it to \emph{Serial} and the \emph{Plotter}. Running this code on my laptop and turning the knob on the potentiometer produces this output. My potentiometer has a very large knob on the front and is easy to turn. Some potentiometers have a small screw on top that you need to turn with a screwdriver. Turning the screw or the knob results in chaning the resistance and therefore changing the voltage read by the CPX.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Reading an analog signal in Mu with Serial monitor and Plotter open}{voltage-3-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/analogio_mu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
For this lab I want you to spin the potentiometer all the way to one side and then the other while recording time and the analog value. I then want you to plot the data with time on the x-axis and voltage on the y-axis. Remember to convert a digital output to voltage you just need to use the equation below where D is the raw value from the analog port. 3.3V is the range of the ADC and \(2^{16}\) is the maximum value the ADC can represent.%
\begin{equation}
V = \frac{3.3D}{2^{16}}\label{voltage-3-10-2}
\end{equation}
%
\par
After doing this experiment myself, this is the plot I obtain. The code is not provided as reading data and plotting has been discussed in a previous lab (See \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}). From the screenshot though you can see how I convert the digital output to an analog signal.%
\par
\alert{NOTE THAT ON LINE 6 IT READS}%
\begin{preformatted}
time -= time[0]
\end{preformatted}
\alert{Notice the minus sign in front of the equal sign. That effects a lot.}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Plotting analog signal in volts in Thonny}{voltage-3-15}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/plotting_analogio.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}

\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Pitot Probe}{}{Pitot Probe}{}{}{pitot}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{pitot-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Alligator Clips (x3)%
\item{}\href{https://www.amazon.com/Pressure-MPXV7002DP-Breakout-Transducer-Components/dp/B08C5CF7CB}{\linkhilite{Pressure Transducer}} + \href{https://www.amazon.com/Jadeshay-Differential-Speedometer-Precision-Instrument/dp/B07Q6126B6/ref=pd_bxgy_thbs_d_sccl_1/131-3143700-2176464?pd_rd_w=7zZaR\&content-id=amzn1.sym.9bef5913-5870-4504-8883-3ba89d7f8e39\&pf_rd_p=9bef5913-5870-4504-8883-3ba89d7f8e39\&pf_rd_r=KSZ04S642T1YZ2QXTHWK\&pd_rd_wg=MrPsd\&pd_rd_r=63018516-b34d-43f9-9369-36d494eda7a3\&pd_rd_i=B07Q6126B6\&psc=1}{\linkhilite{Pitot Probe}}%
\item{}Breadboard%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{pitot-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Understand how pitot probes works%
\item{}Understand the relationship between a voltage signal from a pitot probe to a pressure value%
\item{}Understand the relationship between pressure and windspeed%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
Although a CPX has numerous sensors built in, you can easily augment the capabilities of the CPX using either I2C or just the ADC on board the CPX. In this lab, if you purchased a \href{https://www.amazon.com/Hobbypower-Airspeed-MPXV7002DP-Differential-controller/dp/B00WSFWO36/ref=sr_1_3?dchild=1\&keywords=Airspeed+sensor+kit\&qid=1590532161\&sr=8-3}{\linkhilite{pitot probe}} you will be able to do this assignment\hyperlink{pitot-amazon}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 40}]}}. Since you don't need the pitot probe for very long you can always borrow one from some other team or from your instructor if they have one. Let's talk about the hardware and the wiring to get this to work. First, pitot probes work by mechanically changing the dynamic pressure of the incoming airflow as shown in the \href{https://makersportal.com/blog/2019/02/06/arduino-pitot-tube-wind-speed-theory-and-experiment}{\linkhilite{figure below}} (Courtesy of \href{https://makersportal.com/blog?author=59b036fc6073554c1cfffef7}{\linkhilite{Joshua Hrisko}}) \hyperlink{pitot-author}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 39}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Schematic of Pitot probe showing static and dynamic pressures\hyperlink{pitot-author}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 39}]}}}{pitot-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pitot_tube_drawing_transparent_cleaner.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The pitot probe has two pressure taps that measure static (ambient) pressure and dynamic (stagnation) pressure. These taps move through two silicon tubes to a pressure transducer that has a strain gauge that separates both pressures. When the pressure on one side of the transducer is larger than the other, it will flex the membrane and create strain. This strain runs through a wheatstone bridge with a voltage offset to the pin labeled analog. The analog signal from the analog pin will be denoted as \(V_{pitot}\). The pressure transducer used in this lab is the MPXV7002 which is a differential pressure sensor. The \href{https://www.nxp.com/docs/en/data-sheet/MPXV7002.pdf}{\linkhilite{datasheet}} indicates that the voltage offset of the pitot probe is 2.5V and the change in voltage is proportional to the change in pressure in units of kPa\hyperlink{pitot-datasheet}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 41}]}}. This means that \(\Delta P\) is given by the equation below.%
\begin{equation}
\Delta P_{kPa} = V_{pitot} - 2.5\label{pitot-2-5-5}
\end{equation}
remembering that \(V_{pitot}=3.3D_o/65535\). Also note that 2.5V is the nominal voltage even though your sensor might have something slightly different like 2.4V or 2.6V. To measure wind speed you can use a variation of Bernoulli's principle. Note it is necessary to convert the pressure \(P\) from kilopascals (kPa) to Pascals (Pa) by multiplying by \(1000\) before using it in this formula.%
\begin{equation}
U = \sqrt{\frac{2 \Delta P_{Pa}}{\rho}}\label{pitot-2-5-9}
\end{equation}
where \(U\) is the wind speed in \(m/s\), \(\Delta P\) is the differential pressure (in Pascals), and \(\rho=1.225~kg/m^3\) is the density of air. As for wiring up the circuit itself, the transducer has 3 pins, +5V, GND and Analog. The figure below shows the transducer connected to an Arduino (Courtesy of \href{https://makersportal.com/blog/2019/02/06/arduino-pitot-tube-wind-speed-theory-and-experiment?srsltid=AfmBOoqrja9WFwnJNLPOLxPEG5mRa1XqM8iP86s0ZYy5Qb1aSA-GpU0D}{\linkhilite{Joshua Hrisko}})\hyperlink{pitot-author}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 39}]}}. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino connected to a pressure transducer\hyperlink{pitot-author}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 39}]}}}{pitot-2-5-16}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino+MPXV7002DP+Wiring+for+Differential+Pressure+Measurement.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
%
\par
It is pretty straightforward to wire this up to a CPX\slash{}CPB as is shown in the wiring diagram below. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pitot probe wired to a CPX}{pitot-2-6-1}{}%
\begin{image}{0.05}{0.9}{0.05}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pitot_wiring.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 Remember that +5V needs to go to VOUT, GND to GND and Analog to any analog pin. Continue to read on to setup your own CPX\slash{}CPB and complete this assignment%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 14.1 Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setup}{}{Setup}{}{}{setup}
To complete this lab, I chose pin A2 as shown in the Figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPlayground Express connected to Pitot Probe and Transducer}{setup-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.05}{0.9}{0.05}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pitot_probe_circuit.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
At that point it's very simple to just print the analog signal in bits to Serial. I've done this below. The code is the same \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Analog/analog_simple.py}{\linkhilite{analog code}} that we've used in the past\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Analogio code in CircuitPython}{setup-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.25}{0.5}{0.25}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/analogio.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The goal of the experiment is to take pitot probe data for 15 seconds with no wind, then 15 seconds of data with a fan on and then 15 seconds of no wind data. You'll need to use one of the datalogging methods (See \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}) to log both time and pitot probe analog value. Once you have that data, import the data on your desktop computer and convert the signal to windspeed as explained above. Using your data, create a plot of windspeed with time on the x-axis and windspeed on the y-axis. Some steps that might help you as you complete this project. First, have Mu plot the voltage coming from the pitot probe. If you've done everything right it will not be zero. The data sheet says there's an offset voltage of 2.5V so you will hopefully get something around 50,000 when you don't blow into the pitot probe. 50,000 multiplied by 3.3\slash{}\(2^{16}\) is around 2.5V. Make a note of that average value you get so you can subtract it off later. Once you've verified you're reading the pitot probe correctly, blow into the pitot probe and using the Plotter or Serial, verify that the analog signal increases. If the signal decreases, it means the pressure taps on the pressure transducer are backwards and you need to flip them. Either that or just flip the sign in your plotting routine on your computer but flipping the tubes might be easier for you. Hopefully when you do this lab you will get some data that looks like this. In this Figure you'll see that when the fan wasn't running the signal was something around 49,800 which is fine. It means your bias is around 2.5 volts. Every pitot probe and circuit will be different. You can then convert this signal to voltage then and then pressure and then finally wind speed.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example pitot probe data showing fan off\slash{}on\slash{}off scenario}{setup-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pitot_probe_data.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code to accomplish this is relatively simple and a portion of the code is shown below. You'll see that when I subtracted the bias from the voltage I also zeroed out any negative values. That is, any delta voltage less than zero was set to zero. A couple of things about this chart. The data from the pitot probe is super noisy which means attaching a complementary filter is probably a good idea provided you don't over filter the signal and run into aliasing issues (See \hyperref[aliasing]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{aliasing}}}}). You can see that I implemented an offline complementary filter and plotted it in the orange line which helps the noise issue quite a bit. You'll also notice that the noise is about 2 m\slash{}s. It turns out that pitot probes are actually not very accurate lower than about 2 m\slash{}s. They would be great for an airplane or you driving down the highway but they wouldn't be very good to take wind data outside on a calm day.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Example windspeed data showing complementary filter in Thonny}{setup-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pitot_probe_final.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 14.2 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%

I've also done this pitot project before and have posted a video on Youtube about \href{https://youtu.be/jSLIRC1cfvE}{\linkhilite{Converting Pitot Probe Data to Windspeed}}. \alert{There is a typo in the video though. V1 is supposed to have a sqrt())}. I also have a video about \href{https://youtu.be/zTGa4sk6UZE}{\linkhilite{complementary filters}} and another about \href{https://youtu.be/8F_8st_8MmA}{\linkhilite{aliasing issues}} \hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%

\typeout{Section 14.3 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{pitot-5}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to wire up a pitot probe and record time and raw analog signal from the pitot probe as you turn a fan on and off. My suggestion is you record at least 30 seconds of data with the fan off and then 30 seconds with the fan on and then again 30 seconds with the fan off. Specific requirements are shown below.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction with wiring diagram - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}\alert{In addition to the standard format above, you must also return the pitot probe you borrowed in class - Pass\slash{}Fail}%
\item{}Include a photo of your circuit with your pitot probe wired up to an analog pin - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu with the Plotter open showing the raw analog signal. The Mu code also needs to show the same analog pin as the circuit above - 10\%%
\item{}Include a plot of the raw analog signal vs time that clearly shows when the fan is on and off - 20\%%
\item{}Convert your raw signal to windspeed and filter your signal using an offline complementary filter. Plot both unfiltered and filtered windspeed on the same plot and include a legend. Make sure you add the saturation filter to prevent a negative in the square root - 20\%%
\item{}What is the maximum windspeed that the CPX\slash{}CPB can read? - 10\%%
\item{}The data sheet also suggests that you use a \(470~pF\) capacitor to filter the output. Select two resistors for a low pass filter such that the DC Gain would be equal to 1. Assume the total impedance of both resistors is \(2~k\Omega\). Also compute the cutoff frequency. - 10\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Include 2 vidoes: First, film your entire team and your screen as you show the digital output change in the Plotter in Mu as the freestream airflow changes. Then, include a video of you plotting your raw digital output and converting that to windspeed on your computer - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
