\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 20 Time Constant of a Thermistor}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Time Constant of a Thermistor}{}{Time Constant of a Thermistor}{}{}{thermistor}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{thermistor-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Laptop%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{thermistor-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Learn the basic form of a first order system%
\item{}Learn the basic solution of a first order system%
\item{}Understand settling time and how that effects engineering%
\item{}Applied Estimation of a First Order system%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
For this lab we're going to use the thermistor located on Pin A9 of the CPX\slash{}CPB. In a previous lab we measured the analog signal from the pin and also used the \emph{adafruit\textunderscore{}thermistor.py} module that converts the voltage from the thermistor to celsius (See \hyperref[temperature-sensor]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{temperature-sensor}}}}).%
\par
For this lab though, we're not only going to measure the temperature from the thermistor, we're going to analyze the dynamics of the thermistor and how it responds to an abrupt change in temperature. It is said that a themistor can be modeled as a first order system. The basic form of a first order system can be shown below.%
\begin{equation}
\dot{T} = \sigma (T_f - T)\label{thermistor-2-4-1}
\end{equation}
where \(T\) is our state variable and \(\dot{T}\) is the derivative of our state. For this example let's assume that \(T\) is temperature. In this case \(T_f\) is an external temperature that is either higher or lower than current temperature causing the derivative of temperature to be non-zero. You can see in this case that once the temperature of the system is equal to the external forcing temperature, the derivative of the temperature of the system goes to zero. This implies that the system has reached equilibrium. The variable \(\sigma\) has units of \(Hz\) or \(1/sec\). The inverse of \(\sigma\) is \(\tau\) the time constant.%
\begin{equation}
\tau = \frac{1}{\sigma}\label{thermistor-2-4-11}
\end{equation}
%
\par
The time constant of the system is related to how quickly the system responds to change or even how long it takes for the system to reach equilibrium. Reaching equilibrium quantitatively happens when the state has changed 98\%. In other words the state of the system is within 2\% of the equilibrium state. This is called the settling time \(t_s\). The time constant is related to the settling time using the equation below.%
\begin{equation}
\tau = \frac{t_s}{4}\label{thermistor-2-5-2}
\end{equation}
%
\par
The only question now of course is what is the solution \(T(t)\) or rather the temperature as a function of time. In this case, the solution to the above dynamics equation can be solved using \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VOv2HI3i7oo}{\linkhilite{standard first order differential equation techniques}} to obtain the solution below\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\begin{equation}
T(t) = (T_0-T_f)e^{-\sigma t} + T_f\label{e_thermistor}
\end{equation}
%
\par
Looking at the equation now you can see some properties right away. When \(t=0\), the first term reduces to \((T_0-T_f)\) which means the initial temperature is \(T_0\) or the initial temperature. When \(t\rightarrow\infty\), the first term drops to zero and thus the temperature is \(T_f\) or final temperature. In this lab we're going to get the time constant of the \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermistor}{\linkhilite{thermistor}} on board the CPX\hyperlink{thermistor-wikipedia}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 63}]}}. If you take data on the CPX and walk outside or place the thermistor directly into a fridge, the temperature change will not be immediate. In order to estimate the time constant of a thermistor you need to change the temperature somehow. Blelow are some ideas for how to change the temperature of the CPX\slash{}CPB. You can do one or more of these methods to get data for this lab but you're only required to pick one.%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Start logging data and then place the CPX into the fridge%
\item{}Put the CPX into the fridge and then pull the CPX out of the fridge and watch the temperature return to ambient%
\item{}Walk outside on a hot (or cold) day and watch the CPX change temperature due to your A\slash{}C.%
\item{}Walk inside and watch the CPX get warmer (or colder) as your HVAC changes the CPX temperature.%
\item{}Use a hair dryer to heat up the CPX and then watch it cool down.%
\end{enumerate}
%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 20.1 Initial Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Initial Setup}{}{Initial Setup}{}{}{thermistor-3}
There is no wiring diagram for this project because we're using the thermistor that's already on the CPX\slash{}CPB. The code to read the thermistor is pretty simple and is shown below. Remember that the thermistor is a resistor that changes with temperature. The ADC on the CPX converts the voltage across the thermistor to temperature. \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/thermistor/circuitpython}{\linkhilite{The Adafruit Learn system}} does a bit of work to explain the conversion from voltage to temperature and is also explained in \hyperref[temperature-sensor]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{temperature-sensor}}}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. Just remember that the thermistor on pin A9 is connected to the high side of the voltage divider which effects the equations to compute the resistance and voltage in the thermistor. My version of the code is also on my \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Temp/record_temperature_thermistor.py}{\linkhilite{Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. \alert{A simple version of the code is shown below but note that the code is from a much earlier and simpler version of my code which only reads the temperature in Celsius. The code on my Github has more functionality such as printing the raw ADC value and the voltage.}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Read thermistor data code in CircuitPython}{thermistor-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/thermistor.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 20.2 Estimating the Time Constant}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Estimating the Time Constant}{}{Estimating the Time Constant}{}{}{thermistor-4}
I did the first two examples (using a fridge) and plotted both data sets in the same script as shown below. I opted to use method 1 (see \hyperref[daq]{\linkhilite{chapter~{\xreffont\ref{daq}}}}) from the datalogging project and just have the data print to Serial and then unplug the CPX when I'm done taking data and copy and paste the data into a text file.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Heating and cooling examples plotted in Thonny}{thermistor-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/thermistor_plots.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
At this point it's possible to get the time constant by remembering that the settling time (time it takes the temperature to settle out) is equal to 4 times the time constant (\(\tau\)) and thus the time constant is the settling time divided by 4. After computing the settling time for both data sets and overlaying the equations on the measured data I get these two plots.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Exponential regression curves fit to heating and cooling data in Thonny}{thermistor-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/thermistor_plots2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
What was interesting was that the time constant for heating up was 62.5 seconds and for cooling down it was 155 seconds. The time to get cold was way slower than heating up. I'm not a heat transfer expert so I won't comment as to why this happened. One other import note I'd like to mention is the cool down phase was much more accurate than the heat up phase. This is most likely because when I pulled the thermistor out of the fridge I touched it with my hands and then moved it to a table. There was also alot more airflow outside the fridge which would change the overall dynamics. Still, the fitted data matches up pretty well and I hope yours does too.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 20.3 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{thermistor-5}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment, you are to change the ambient temperature of the CPX\slash{}CPB through heating or cooling and then record time and voltage while waiting for the CPX\slash{}CPB to return to ambient temperature. You are to then plot voltage, resistance and temperature as a function of time and then using the graph of temperature (not using a curve fit), estimate \(T_0\), \(T_f\),\(\sigma\),\(t_s\) and \(\tau\). Finally, using \hyperref[e_thermistor]{\linkhilite{equation~({\xreffont\ref{e_thermistor}})}}, plot the estimate of \(T(t)\).%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Take a picture of you actively heating or actively cooling the CPX\slash{}CPB - 10\%%
\item{}Plot the raw voltage and resistance in the thermistor vs time - 20\%%
\item{}Plot your temperature data with time on the x-axis and temperature on the y-axis and overlay your fitted data on top of your measured data. Points will be given based on how well your fit is - 20\%%
\item{}Include a table of your estimated parameters - 20\%%
\item{}Using the time constant you computed, assume you accidentally put your CPX\slash{}CPB in the oven at 400F. Simulate your system heating up from room temperatue (25C) to 400F and tell me how long I would have before I destroy my CPX\slash{}CPB. Make sure to show your work or you receive no credit - 10\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your screen as you explain how you heated or cooled the CPX\slash{}CPB. Then show the thermistor running on your computer with the Serial Monitor open in Mu. Finally plot the regression line over your plotted data on your computer - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 21 Second Order Systems and Aliasing}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Second Order Systems and Aliasing}{}{Second Order Systems and Aliasing}{}{}{second-order}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{second-order-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}Some sort of oscillating system like a swinging pendulum or vibrating ruler%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{second-order-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Learn the basic form of a second order system%
\item{}Understand the difference between underdamped, critically damped, and overdamped%
\item{}Understand natural frequency and damping ratio%
\item{}Applied Estimation of a Second Order system%
\item{}Understand the pitfalls of aliasing%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
A second order system undergoing free motion will have dynamics that look like this%
\begin{equation}
\ddot{\theta} + 2\zeta \omega_n \dot{\theta} + {\omega_n}^2\label{second-order-2-3-1}
\end{equation}
%
\par
In this case, the solution to the above equation can be solved using \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VOv2HI3i7oo}{\linkhilite{standard second order differential equation techniques}} to obtain the solution below \hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\begin{equation}
\theta(t) = \theta_0e^{-\sigma t}cos(\omega_d t)\label{e_secondorder}
\end{equation}
%
\begin{equation}
\omega_n = \sqrt{\sigma^2 + {\omega_d}^2}\label{second-order-2-4-4}
\end{equation}
%
\begin{equation}
\zeta = \frac{\sigma}{\omega_n}\label{second-order-2-4-5}
\end{equation}
%
\par
Looking at the equations you can see that if the time series of the oscillations are known, the damping constant and damped natural frequency can be obtained. These two values can be combined to obtain the natural frequency and the damping ratio. So let's get some data.%
\par
What I'd like you to do for this lab is to find some sort of parameter that varies in some sort of sinusoidal way. I've come up with a few ideas below. You may pick anyone you want although some are easier than others.%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Drive over a speed bump - If you drive over a speed bump slowly and place your CPX on the dashboard your car will hopefully vibrate for a few seconds after you drive over it. Your acceleration will look somewhat like a sine wave.%
\item{}Vibrate a ruler - If you place a ruler on the edge of a desk and deflect it, it will vibrate. If you place the CPX on the ruler you'll be able to measure the vibration of the ruler.%
\item{}Attach CPX to a spring or a weight to the end of rubber bands - Take the CPX and attach it to a weight of some kind and attach the weight to a spring or a set of rubber bands. This is a mass spring damper simple and will vibrate at the natural frequency of square root of stiffness divided by mass. I chose to do this example.%
\item{}Build a Pendulum: If you decide to build a pendulum, you need to hang a weight on the CPX or attach the CPX to something heavy. This will make the ratio between cable to end mass much smaller and thus better for data and fitting. An example includes  duct taping your CPX to a water bottle or something. It would also be better to use a string and mount the CPX to the string with an external battery pack and have the CPX log data internally. This way most of the mass would be concentrated at the tip of the pendulum. Another idea is to take a paper towel cardboard tube and tape the cpx inside it with the cable running through it. Then duct a large weight on the end of the paper towel tube and then hinge the top of the tube by skewering a screwdriver through it. This will allow the tube to swing like a pendulum rather than the string.%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
There are most likely many other options so try and get creative and find something oscillatory or dynamic in some way. Try to find something that changes relatively quickly. The temperature outside changes in a sinusoidal fashion but it's so slow it would take you days to do this experiment.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 21.1 Setup Pendulum Example}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Setup Pendulum Example}{}{Setup Pendulum Example}{}{}{second-order-3}
In this example I'm going to swing a pendulum in the X\slash{}Y plane of the accelerometer sensor so that I can ignore the Z axis data. I'm going to get the actual angle of the pendulum but if you're building something else you can ignore this part. In order to convert the accelerometer values to pitch angle, I'm going to use the same equations discussed in the accelerometer lab (See \hyperref[accelerometer]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{accelerometer}}}})%
\par
Remember, on the CPX\slash{}CPB specifically you'll want to import the math module and use the atan2 function. When I swing the pendulum, this is the result I get from Plotter in Mu.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Oscillations shown from atan2 measurement of accelerometer in Mu}{second-order-3-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/oscillation_mu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
If I then bring this into Python I get the following plot below. In my data set I only logged the angle. Since the time step between each point was 0.01 seconds I was able to create a time series. It's pretty clear that there is some nonlinearity in the data so I chose to start the data at 0.5 seconds. Another thing I noticed was that the angle settled out to around 8 degrees so I chose to subtract off that bias from the angle data.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Oscillation data plotted in Thonny}{second-order-3-6}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/oscillation_plots.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 21.2 Estimating Parameters}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Estimating Parameters}{}{Estimating Parameters}{}{}{second-order-4}
After trimming the data and removing some bias it was time to get my damping constant and damped natural frequency. There are a few equations that can help you obtain these parameters. First, the settling time is the length of time it takes for the oscillations to settle. The settling time can be used to find the damping constant. This is equal to:%
\begin{equation}
\sigma = \frac{4}{T_s}\label{second-order-4-2-1}
\end{equation}
%
\par
For my data set the settling time was about 1.25 seconds which gave a damping constant of 3.2. Once I had the damping constant I could obtain the damped natural frequency. This was done by measuring the distance between two peaks in the data set. There is a peak at around 0.5 seconds and another at around 0.95 seconds. I can use this to compute a period T. Period can be computed to angular frequency using the equation below.%
\begin{equation}
\omega_d = \frac{2\pi}{T}\label{second-order-4-3-1}
\end{equation}
%
\par
Using the period in my wave form I obtained a damped natural frequency of about 14.8 rad\slash{}s. Using these values I can plot the simulated data on top of the measured data noting that my initial angle was about 35 degrees minus the bias of 8 degrees. When I first plotted the data I noticed that my fit wasn't entirely perfect. My period was correct but my damping rate was too high. I realized it was because my settling time was too big. I increased the settling time to 1.8 seconds and got this plot here. You can see that my fitted data lined up almost perfectly with my measured data.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Sinusoidal regression curve plotted on top of oscillation data in Thonny}{second-order-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/oscillation_plots2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 21.3 Aliasing}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Aliasing}{}{Aliasing}{}{}{aliasing}
Aliasing occurs when you don't take data fast enough. The speed of taking data to avoid aliasing depends on the natural frequency of the system itself. To show this I changed the length of the pendulum which changed the natural frequency. I then proceeded to measure the angle as it oscillated at its natural frequency. I repeated this at sampling frequencies of 1, 10 and 100 Hz. The way I changed the sampling frequency was by changing the time.sleep value in the while True loop. The \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Accelerometer/low_level_accel.py}{\linkhilite{accelerometer code}} I used can be found on Github\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. After I finished the experiment I had 3 data files that I plotted on top of each other. This what I got with the longest string. It's easy to see in the photo that sampling at 1 Hz was way too slow to capture the natural oscillations of the water bottle. However, 100 Hz and even 10 Hz was plenty fast to sample the oscillations. According to the recorded data there was above 19 cycles in 9 seconds which is about 2 Hz. In this case, as long as we sample at 4 Hz the signal will be captured properly which is why 10 Hz and 100 Hz is able to capture the signal correctly.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Aliasing shown with oscillatory data in Thonny}{aliasing-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/aliasing.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once I sampled my waveform at 3 different sampling rates I elected to shrink the length of the pendulum to change the natural frequency and see if that affected any aliasing I saw. The results are shown in the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Aliasing for shorter pendulum shown in Thonny}{aliasing-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/aliasing2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In this case you can see that there were 33 cycles in 10 seconds which is 3.3 Hz. The Nyquist criteria states that I need to sample at 6.6 Hz. The Nyquist criteria is very specific though in that if you sample at twice the frequency, you will just obtain the correct frequency. This does not mean that you will capture every data point properly. Hence in the chart above, the blue line at 100 Hz is perfect, the green line at 1 Hz is too slow (less than 6.6 Hz) and the orange line at 10 Hz captures the frequency correctly but between 5 and 8 seconds does not adequately capture the waveform. In my opinion, in order to sample the data effectively and not just obtain the frequency of the waveform, you need to sample at 4 times the natural frequency. So for the waveform of 3.3 Hz, the Montalvo frequency would be 13.2 Hz which is higher than the 10 Hz. This explains why the 10 hz sample is not perfect while the 100 hz sample rate is much better.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 21.4 Extra Help}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Extra Help}{}{Extra Help}{}{}{second-order-6}
I have an accompanying youtube video where I discuss \href{https://youtu.be/8F_8st_8MmA}{\linkhilite{aliasing issues}} which might help you better understand aliasing in general\hyperlink{youtube}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 50}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 21.5 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{second-order-7}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to create a system that oscillates and capture data while oscillating. You are to sample the system at 1, 10 and 100 Hz and plot the shifted and cleaned data for all 3 sample rates on the same graph. For the data sampled at 100 Hz you are then to estimate the initial amplitude, the settling time and the period using the graph. Then compute the natural frequency and damping ratio. Finally, using \hyperref[e_secondorder]{\linkhilite{equation~({\xreffont\ref{e_secondorder}})}}, plot the amplitude as a function of time on top of your 100hz sampled data. \alert{Note, if you decide to make a pendulum you must ensure that the CPX\slash{}CPB does not spin when oscillating otherwise you won't be able to use your acceleration data easily.}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your experiment and explain the state that you are measuring - 10\%%
\item{}Plot the results of your data (make sure to connect your data points) vs time for all 3 sampling frequencies (1,10, 100Hz) and include a legend - 10\%%
\item{}Include a table of your estimated parameters as well as the natural frequency and damping ratio - 20\%%
\item{}Plot your measured data sampled at 100hz with your fitted equation on top - 20\%%
\item{}Based on your results and your computed natural frequency, comment on the Nyquist frequency and the Montalvo frequency of your system and whether or not you encountered aliasing in your experiment based on the figures and the Nyquist\slash{}Montalvo frequency - 10\%%
\item{}Using the natural frequency and damping ratio you computed, simulate your system for an initial amplitude of 1 for two times the settling time. - 10\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing the camera the experiment you used to create oscillations in the accelerometer. Then plot your accelerometer data and show the regression line fit over your oscillations - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 22 Inertial Measurement Unit}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Inertial Measurement Unit}{}{Inertial Measurement Unit}{}{}{imu}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{imu-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB or Arduino%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4485}{\linkhilite{LSM6DS33+LIS3MDL}} (Note that other Inertial Measurement or "DOF" Sensors will work for this lab you will just have to change the code to accommodate the change in hardware)\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}%
\item{}Alligator Clips (x4)%
\item{}Bread Board%
\item{}Soldering iron%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{imu-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}See and understand the concept of soldering%
\item{}Understand the I2C protocol at a high level%
\item{}Learn the components of an Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) (Accelerometer, Rate Gyro, Magnetometer)%
\item{}Read IMU data and plot%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
In this lab we're going to use an Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) sensor to measure angular velocity and the magnetic field of the surrounding environment.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) without header pins soldered}{imu-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The sensor above is the \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4485}{\linkhilite{LSM6DS33+LIS3MDL}}\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. You basically have 2 separate microchips in one. The first (LSM6DS33) is a 3-axis accelerometer and 3-axis rate gyro. The first measures acceleration and the second measures angular velocity. The LIS3MDL is a 3-axis magnetometer which measures magnetic fields. The three of these sensors put together (accelerometer, rate gyro, magnetometer) is called an IMU (Inertial Measurement Unit). It's actually possible to measure roll and pitch of an airplane using the accelerometer and heading using the magnetometer. Combining the angular velocity of the rate gyro can create a complete attitude estimation algorithm for six-degree-of-freedom vehicles (6DOF). This sensor does not come standard in the current iteration of the kit but you can purchase one on Adafruit for only \textdollar{}10 at the time of this writing. The interesting thing about this device is that you can actually purchase the LSM6DS33 and LIS3MDL separately but this breakout board has both chips on board. The goal of this lab is not only to use this specific sensor but to understand IMUs and I2C protocol.Most if not all breakout boards on the Adafruit website use I2C communication. You'll know if the breakout board uses I2C if you find SDA\slash{}SCL pins on the board. It will also say it in the quick description of the sensor.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{IMU on Adafruit}{imu-2-6}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu2.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The nice thing about this sensor is that there is a magnetometer on board. Recall that the accelerometer can be used to measure roll and pitch (See \hyperref[accelerometer]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{accelerometer}}}}). By combining the magnetometer in this IMU, it's possible to get the compass heading of the sensor.%
%
\begin{equation}
\psi = tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\bar{\beta}_z s_{\phi} - \bar{\beta}_y c_{\phi}}{\bar{\beta}_x c_{\phi} + \bar{\beta}_y s_{\theta}s_{\phi} + \bar{\beta}_z c_{\phi}s_{\theta}}\right)\label{imu-2-8}
\end{equation}
In the equations above, \(\beta\) is the magnetic field along all 3 axes. Again shorthand is used for cosine and sine functions and the derivation is left for another course entirely (See \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/LaTeX/tree/master/Aerospace_Mechanics}{\linkhilite{Aerospace Mechanics}})\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. When you open the packaging of this breakout board you'll notice that the header pins are missing. First you'll need to cut a row of 4 and 6 for the top and bottom side of the board and \alert{solder the header pins to the sensor}. If you're taking my class I can solder this for you or teach everyone about soldering during a lecture session of class. If you are taking this class elsewhere you have two options: try and find someone who can solder this real quick (only takes about 5 minutes) or buy your own soldering iron and try to solder yourself. Once the device is soldered you can "plug" it into a breadboard.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 22.1 CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}{}{CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}{}{}{imu-3}
The wiring for this system requires 4 wires. The figure below shows the IMU connected to \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/lis3mdl-triple-axis-magnetometer/python-circuitpython}{\linkhilite{Feather M4}} (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/siddacious}{\linkhilite{Bryan Siepert}})\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Wiring diagram of IMU connected to a FeatherWing\hyperlink{imu-wiring}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 42}]}}}{imu-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/sensors_python_feather_wiring_breadboard.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The only difference between the wiring diagram above and the CPX\slash{}CPB is that you will be using 4 alligator clips. The rest is straightforward. You need \emph{3.3V} to run to \emph{VIN}, \emph{GND} to \emph{GND} and then \emph{SDA} to \emph{SDA} and \emph{SCL} to \emph{SCL}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Photo of IMU Connected to a CircuitPlayground Express}{imu-3-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu_correct.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The photo above uses a combination of alligator clips and male to male wires into a breadboard. The red alligator clip is connected to the \emph{3.3V} pin on the CPB and then also connected to the red male-male wire which is connected to the \emph{VIN} pin on the IMU. The black alligator clip is connected to the \emph{GND} pin on the CPB and also connected to the brown male-male wire which is connected to the \emph{GND} pin on the IMU. The blue alligator clip is connected directly to the \emph{SDA} pins on both the CPB and the IMU. The white alligator clip connected to the yellow male-male wire which is connected to the \emph{SCL} pins on both the CPB and the IMU. Recall that SDA and SCL are external pins for I2C. In the accelerometer lab we used the internal I2C pins to access the accelerometer (See \hyperref[accelerometer]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{accelerometer}}}}). In this lab we're going to use the external I2C pads to connect different I2C sensors.%
\par
Once you have the circuit wired and soldered it's time to work on software. First, you want to make sure you have your \href{https://circuitpython.org/downloads}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python UF2}} up to date\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. In this example I'm using the 6.X version. Once I updated my UF2 I also updated my \href{https://circuitpython.org/libraries}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python Libraries}} (See \hyperref[installing-modules]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{installing-modules}}}} for help on installing extra modules on your CPX\slash{}CPB)\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. The specific modules I needed for this lab were:%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}bus\textunderscore{}device%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}register%
\item{}lis3mdl%
\item{}lsm6ds33%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
Once you have the necessary modules you can run some example code. The Adafruit Learn page has a \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/lsm6ds33-6-dof-imu=accelerometer-gyro/python-circuitpython}{\linkhilite{tutorial for the LSM6DS33}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. The problem with the tutorial is that it seems like it was written for the Raspberry or some other microcontroller. As such I had to find some example code on \href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_CircuitPython_LSM6DS/blob/master/examples/lsm6ds_lsm6ds33_simpletest.py}{\linkhilite{Adafruit's Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. After following both tutorials I was able to make my own script and upload it to my \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Accelerometer/external_lis3mdl_lsm6dss.py}{\linkhilite{Github}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}. \alert{Note that it's a good exercise when attaching I2C devices to run a scan on all connected I2C devices to make sure you can see the device and that you have the correct address (See \hyperref[I2C_scanner]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{I2C_scanner}}}}).}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{CircuitPython code to read an IMU}{imu-3-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu_code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the code above lines 1-6 import all the modules with line 5 importing the accelerometer on board the CPX and line 6 importing the external sensor wired up to SDA and SCL. Line 8 creates an I2C object using the SDA and SCL pins from the alligator clips and line 11 creates the sensor object. I also include lines 14-17 to include the onboard accelerometer. Notice I can access both sensors no problem. In the while loop line 20 checks the accelerometer on the CPX, line 21 checks the accelerometer on the breakout board and line 22 checks the angular velocity on the breakout board. Lines 23 and 24 print to serial and output to the plotter. Note that some lines are commented out because I wanted to try one thing at a time. With both accelerometers printing to the Plotter I could move the CPX and the breakout board in unison and get the following output.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial monitor open showing IMU data in Mu}{imu-3-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu_mu.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Notice that there are 6 numbers printed and 6 lines on the plotter. Both the CPX and the breakout board have little XYZ cartesian coordinate systems. I had to line them up properly before I started moving them. My suggestion would be for you to get some hot glue or 3M tape and place both breakout board and CPX on some sort of hard material like plywood, masonite, or even a cutting board. Anything to keep everything together.%
\par
Once you've done this, try uncommenting the line of code that prints the angular velocity. When I do that and move the breakout board around I can measure the angular velocity of each axis. The units are in radians per second but it's pretty obvious just from the magnitude of the graph.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Angular Velocity data of IMU shown in Serial Monitor or Mu}{imu-3-14}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu_mag.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The final part is to get the LIS3MDL (magnetometer) to work. The starting point for me was the \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/lis3mdl-triple-axis-magnetometer/python-circuitpython}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Learn page}}, along with the simple example from \href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_CircuitPython_LIS3MDL/blob/master/examples/lis3mdl_simpletest.py}{\linkhilite{Adafruit's Github}}. After that I was able to create \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Accelerometer/external_lis3mdl.py}{\linkhilite{my own code}}\hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. The only difference in your code is that the address will be 0x1c instead of 0x1e.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Magnetometer code in CircuitPython}{imu-3-16}{}%
\begin{image}{0.1}{0.8}{0.1}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/imu_code_mag.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code is almost identical to the code before except all the LIS3DH and LSM6DS33 code is commented out. Instead I have code to grab the magnetometer (LIS3MDL) at address 0x1E. Line 25 then calls the magnetometer and prints it.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 22.2 Arduino Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{}{imu-4}
Wiring this to the Arduino requires the same setup as the CPX\slash{}CPB and also uses I2C. From the Arduino to the accelerometer, the \emph{3.3V} pin runs to \emph{VIN}, \emph{GND} to \emph{GND}, \emph{SDA} to \emph{SDA} and \emph{SCL} to \emph{SCL}. This is shown in the figure below. Note that the SD card breakout board from \hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}} is also used in this example to showcase how easy it is to send accelerometer data to an SD card%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Accelerometer connected to an Arduino}{imu-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Accelerometer_Arduino.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Moving on to software, the \emph{.ino} file for both the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33.ino}{\linkhilite{accelerometer}} by itself and the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33_SD/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33_SD.ino}{\linkhilite{accelerometer writing to an SD card}} are both on Github. For both of the versions of the code you need to install some libraries. You'll notice that at the top of the code there is a \#include statement which adds a specific library.%
\begin{preformatted}
#include <Adafruit_LSM6DS33.h>
Adafruit_LSM6DS33 lsm6ds33;
\end{preformatted}
In this case you need the \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}LSM6DS33} library which also requires other libraries in addition to that single \#include call. Also, since the libraries are from Adafruit rather than Arduino you need to navigate to Github and download a few zip libraries. The list of libraries you need and links to those websites are shown below%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/adafruit_lsm6ds}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}LSM6DS}}%
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_BusIO}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}BusIO}}%
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/adafruit_sensor}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}Sensor}}%
\end{enumerate}
Once you have the zip folders downloaded you need to navigate to Sketch\textgreater{}Include Library\textgreater{}Add .ZIP Library... and then add the ZIP file you just downloaded. This is different than adding a library directly from the Arduino website as is done for the SD library in \hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}}. Once you've got the code downloaded and the libraries installed you should be able to compile without seeing any errors. After uploading the code the serial monitor should produce something similar to the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial Monitor Output of Accelerometer Code on Arduino}{imu-4-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Accel_Serial_Output.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the figure above, the sensor is outputtting temperature, acceleration x,y and z as well as the angular velocity along the x,y, and z axes. If you then compile the code which contains the SD card setup and writing you'll be able to write the data to an external SD card which would be super helpful for a standalone project. Again setting up the SD card is done in \hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}}%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Accelerometer Data File on SD Card}{imu-4-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Accelerometer_Data_File.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the figure above, the code only writes the millis timer and acceleration data to the SD card but it would be very simple to add temperature and angular velocity to the code.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 22.3 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{imu-5}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to wire up the external IMU and get data from it. You need to mount the CPX\slash{}CPB and the IMU to some sort of hard surface so that when you move the CPX\slash{}CPB the IMU moves as well. Make sure the axis of the IMU and the CPX\slash{}CPB are oriented in the same direction. With system mounted to a hard surface, perform doublet manuevers on each axis for a total of 3 doublets. A doublet is where you rotate the system to +90 degrees and then -90 degrees and then back to zero typically taking around 3 seconds for the entire maneuever. Using the accelerometer, compute the roll and pitch angle in degrees. Then use the magnetometer to compute the yaw angle. Using the roll, pitch and yaw angles, take a derivative to compute the angular velocity. Finally, take the angular velocity data and integrate it to obtain the pitch, roll and yaw angles.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction with wiring diagram - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your CPX\slash{}CPB mounted to a hard surface with the IMU on a breadboard. Be sure to explain in your description about your axis system for both sensors. - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu showing the Plotter open and all 3 angular velocity axes - 10\%%
\item{}Include a plot of both accelerometers for the 3 doublet manuevers. - 10\%%
\item{}Include a plot of angular velocity data for the 3 doublet manuevers. Also plot the derivative of the pitch, roll and yaw angles on top of this plot and add a legend to clearly indicate which is which. - 20\%%
\item{}Include a plot of magnetometer data for the 3 doublet maneuevers - 10\%%
\item{}Plot the roll, pitch and yaw angles in degrees from the accelerometer\slash{}magnetometer as well as the integrated rate gyro angles. Add a legend to clearly indicate which line is which. - 20\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing the acceleration and angular velocity values change in the Plotter in Mu - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 23 Barometers}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Barometers}{}{Barometers}{}{}{pressure}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{pressure-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB or Arduino%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4633}{\linkhilite{LPS22}} (Note that other barometers will work for this lab you will just have to change the code to accommodate the change in hardware)\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}%
\item{}Alligator Clips (x4)%
\item{}Bread Board%
\item{}Soldering iron%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{pressure-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Continue to understand the concept of soldering%
\item{}Reinforce the I2C protocol at a high level%
\item{}Learn the components of a Pressure and temperature Sensor (Barometer)%
\item{}Read Pressure data and plot%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
In this lab we're going to use a pressure and temperature sensor (Barometer) to measure atmospheric pressure and temperature. Although \hyperref[temperature-sensor]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{temperature-sensor}}}} already discussed temperature sensors, this external device (LPS22) has a pressure sensor as well adding the ability to measure more atmospheric conditions. Assuming a standard atmosphere model it's also possible to convert pressure to altitude.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Sensor (Barometer) without header pins soldered}{pressure-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/lps22.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The sensor above is the \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4633}{\linkhilite{LPS22}}\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. The first sensor on board is a pressure sensor which uses a pressure transducer (something that creates a voltage when pressure is applied). The second is a temperature sensor which is more than likely a thermistor. The two of these sensors put together (pressure, temperature) is called a Barometer. This can bed purchased on Adafruit for only \textdollar{}7 at the time of this writing. The interesting thing about this device is that you can actually purchase the LPS22 and the temperature sensor separately but this breakout board has both chips on board. The goal of this lab is not only to use this specific sensor but to understand Barometers and reinforce I2C protocol like has been discussed in previous chapters (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}} and \hyperref[accelerometer]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{accelerometer}}}}).%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 23.1 LPS Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{LPS Setup}{}{LPS Setup}{}{}{pressure-3}
When you open the packaging of this breakout board you'll notice that the header pins are missing. First you'll need to cut a row of 8 header pins and \alert{solder the header pins to the sensor}. If you're taking my class I can solder this for you or teach everyone about soldering during a lecture session of class. If you are taking this class elsewhere you have two options: try and find someone who can solder this real quick (only takes about 5 minutes) or buy your own soldering iron and try to solder yourself. Once the device is soldered you can "plug" it into a breadboard. The wiring for this system requires 4 wires. The figure below shows the pressure sensor connected to a \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/lis3mdl-triple-axis-magnetometer/python-circuitpython}{\linkhilite{Feather M4}} (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/siddacious}{\linkhilite{Bryan Siepert}})\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. Notice that the \emph{SDA\slash{}SCL} pins on the LPS22 are listed \emph{SDI\slash{}SCK} respectively.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Sensor Connected to a Feather M4}{pressure-bryan}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/adafruit_products_d_cp_feather_wiring_bb.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 23.2 CPB Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{CPB Wiring and Software}{}{CPB Wiring and Software}{}{}{pressure-4}
The only difference between the wiring diagram shown previously and the CPX\slash{}CPB is that you will be using 4 alligator clips. The rest is straightforward. You need \emph{3.3V} to run to \emph{VIN}, \emph{GND} to \emph{GND} and then \emph{SDA} to \emph{SDI} and \emph{SCL} to \emph{SCK}. The two figures below show a CPB wired to the LPS22.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Sensor Connected to a CPB}{pressure-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/lps22_CPB.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the photos above and below, the red alligator clip is connected to \emph{3.3V} and is then connected to a red male-male wire which is then connected to the \emph{VIN} pin on the LPS22. The black alligator clip is connected to the \emph{GND} pin which is connected to a brown male-male wire connected to the \emph{GND} pin on the LPS22. The white alligator clip is connected to the \emph{SCL} pin connected to an orange male-male wire connected to the \emph{SCK} pin on the LPS22. Finally, the yellow alligator clip is connected to the \emph{SDA} pin connected to a yellow male-male wire connected to the \emph{SDI} pin on the LPS22.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Sensor Connected to a CPB with a Breadboard}{pressure-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/lps22.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once you have the circuit wired and soldered it's time to work on software. First, you want to make sure you have your \href{https://circuitpython.org/downloads}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python UF2}} up to date\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Once I updated my UF2 I also updated my \href{https://circuitpython.org/libraries}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python Libraries}} (See \hyperref[installing-modules]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{installing-modules}}}} for help on installing extra modules on your CPX\slash{}CPB)\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. In this lab I purposefully made the code a bit more elaborate by adding, bluetooth, and method 3 data logging. The reason is that my students in Aerospace Propulsion and Spacecraft Design have to build a hobbyist level rocket and get pressure data during the flight. This way they know how high their rocket went. As such they need to build a standalone datalogger that can be placed into a rocket. The specific modules I needed for this lab for all of that functionality is as follows:%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}lis3dh%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}lps2x%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}thermistor%
\item{}neopixel%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}ble%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
Notice that I also added the thermistor and lis3dh libraries to get acceleration and temperature from the CPB. Once you have the necessary modules you can run some example code. \alert{Note that it's a good exercise when attaching I2C devices to run a scan on all connected I2C devices to make sure you can see the device and that you have the correct address (See \hyperref[I2C_scanner]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{I2C_scanner}}}}).} Once you're positive the I2C address of the LPS22 is setup properly you can run the example code to \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/tree/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/Pressure_Accel_Datalogging}{\linkhilite{log pressure and acceleration data}}. Note that the link previously shown is to a folder of many many different pieces of software. The reason is because some students have a CPX that doesn't have bluetooth, some students want their CPX\slash{}CPB to beep and some students use a BME280 instead of an LPS22 to log data. Make sure to select the correct version for your hardware. Once you get it working the output will look something like this.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Data in Mu}{pressure-4-8}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Pressure_Logging.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The data shown have multiple columns of data which are time,x accel,y accel,z accel,pressure,relative humidity,lps22 temperature, CPX\slash{}CPB temperature. Notice that the relative humidity is 0.0 because the LPS22 doesn't have a humidity sensor. If you were to use a BME280 instead of an LPS22 you would get relative humidity data as well. It's possible to convert the pressure data to altitude and is shown in \hyperref[pressure-altitude]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{pressure-altitude}}}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 23.3 Arduino Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{}{pressure-5}
The wiring for the Arduino is similar to any I2C setup like the external IMU (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}). In this case \emph{3.3V} and \emph{GND} are straightforward while \emph{SDI} and \emph{SCK} pins are connected to the dedicated \emph{SDA} and \emph{SCL} pins on the Arduino. The good news is that the entire Arduino family has dedicated \emph{SDA} and \emph{SCL} pins for I2C. The Figure below shows the pressure sensor connected to an Arduino MEGA.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA hooked up to an LPS22, IMU and SD Card breakout board}{pressure-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_LPS_Accel_Hardware.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The Figure above also has an external IMU (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}) and micro sd card breakout board (\hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}}) connected to the Arduino to build a standalone datalogger capable of measuring acceleration and pressure and log that to a micro SD card. This is so students can place this whole setup into a rocket and measure pressure and acceleration while logging that to an SD card. The LPS22 sensor is the sensor on the left but unfortunately has header pins soldered upside down. As such the LPS22 sensor is upside down. Since you can't see the pins I placed another identical LPS22 right below it so the pins are shown. In this case, the blue wire is connected to the same \emph{3.3V} pin as the IMU, the left most green wire is connected to \emph{GND} on the Arduino, the next green wire is the \emph{SCK} pin connected to the same \emph{SCL} pin as the IMU, and the last dark green wire is connected to the \emph{SDI} pin on the LPS22 while the other end is connected to the same \emph{SDA} pin as the IMU. Note that I2C has multiple \textquotedblleft{}addresses\textquotedblright{}. This means you can have multiple different I2C sensors connected to the same \emph{SDA\slash{}SCL} pins and the code\slash{}hardware will know which sensor is where just like addresses on a street. Moving on to software, the code is similar to any I2C device in that it is necessary to get the LP22 library but also \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}BusIO} and \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}Sensor}. Since these libraries are from Adafruit rather than Arduino I find it easier to navigate to Github and download the zip libraries directly. The list of libraries you need and links to those websites are shown below%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_LPS2X}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}LPS2X}}%
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_BusIO}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}BusIO}}%
\item{}\href{https://github.com/adafruit/adafruit_sensor}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}Sensor}}%
\end{enumerate}
Once you have the zip folders downloaded you need to navigate to Sketch\textgreater{}Include Library\textgreater{}Add .ZIP Library... and then add the ZIP file you just downloaded. This is different than adding a library directly from the Arduino website as is done for the SD library in \hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}}. The code for just reading the pressure sensor can be found in the example set of \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}LPS2X} and is done by navigating the toolbar as shown below.%
\begin{preformatted}
File>Examples>Adafruit_LPS2X>adafruit_lps2x_test.
\end{preformatted}
Just in case you're having trouble finding the example script, I've placed a \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/LPS22_Test/LPS22_Test.ino}{\linkhilite{streamlined LPS22 copy}} of the example on Github. A snippet of that code is shown below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Code for LPS22 Only}{pressure-5-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LPS22_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Notice that lines 2-4 of the software have the \emph{\#include} calls to the libraries just installed. \emph{Wire.h} is a pre-installed header file and \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}BusIO} is called from \emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}Sensor} so you the code doesn't require an excplicit include. Once you've got the code opened and the libraries installed you should be able to compile without seeing any errors. After uploading the code the serial monitor should produce something similar to the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial Monitor Output for LPS22 Only}{pressure-5-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/LPS22_Arduino_Output.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Again, it's possible to convert pressure to altitude and is done in \hyperref[pressure-altitude]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{pressure-altitude}}}}. Along with getting pressure data from the LPS22, it's possible to augment the software to also read the external IMU from I2C and log all data to the micro SD card. A snippet of the software is shown below while the software itself for a \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33_Pressure_LPS22_SD/Accelerometer_LSM6DS33_Pressure_LPS22_SD.ino}{\linkhilite{standalone Pressure+IMU datalogger}} is on Github us usual.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Code of IMU, Pressue and SD Card Breakout boards}{pressure-5-13}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Accel_LPS22_Arduino_MEGA_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In that snippet of code, you can see the loop routine where the IMU and the Pressure sensors are polled and printed to the serial monitor together. Once that code is compiled and flashed to the Arduino the serial monitor output will create something similar to the figure below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial Monitor Output for LPS22 and IMU}{pressure-5-15}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Pressure_Accel_Output_Arduino.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 23.4 Converting Pressure to Altitude}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Converting Pressure to Altitude}{}{Converting Pressure to Altitude}{}{}{pressure-altitude}
The pressure data obtained from the CPX\slash{}CPB is in hectopascals (hPa) which is the same as millibars (mbar). The standard atmospheric pressure at sea level is 1013.25 hPa. As you go up in altitude the pressure decreases. The equation to convert pressure to altitude assuming a standard atmosphere is as follows:%
\begin{equation}
h = \frac{1.0-(\frac{p}{p_0}^{1.0/5.25588})}{2.2557 \times 10^{-5.0}}\label{pressure-altitude-2-1}
\end{equation}
where \emph{p} is pressure and \(p_0\) is the standard atmospheric pressure at sea level (1013.25 hPa). This equation assumes a standard atmosphere and is only accurate up to about 11 km in altitude. Beyond that the equation becomes less accurate and you would need to use a more complex model of the atmosphere to get accurate altitude data. However, for our purposes of launching a hobbyist rocket, this equation will work just fine. It's also possible to convert altitude to velocity using a first order derivative.%
%
\begin{equation}
v = \frac{h_{i+1}-h_i}{t_{i+1}-t_i}\label{pressure-altitude-3}
\end{equation}
In order to get that equation to have reasonable results, students typically have to heavily filter their data given the amount of noise in the data. The equation to convert to velocity is shown below but the results and filtering are left to the reader as an assignment. I think students have had a lot of success using moving average and complimentary filters. I'm sure Google and AI tools will be able to help get that working.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 23.5 Data from a Rocket Launch}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Data from a Rocket Launch}{}{Data from a Rocket Launch}{}{}{pressure-7}
My students have placed this entire data logger into a rocket and launched it. The figure below shows the rocket on the launch pad in Samson,AL.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Rocket on the Launch Pad}{pressure-7-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/rocket.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The datalogger itself is attached to a piece of wood that is called a sled and then placed into the rocket prior to launch.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Datalogger Attached to Sled}{pressure-7-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pressure_sled.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the figure above the small gray piece below the CPB is a small 1S Lipo battery and the LPS22 is on the other side of the sled. The figure below shows pressure as a function of time.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Pressure Data from a Rocket Launch}{pressure-7-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/pressure_rocket.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
It's clear from the data that pressure drops as the flight continues showing the boost phase of the rocket. There is a small oscillation near the apogee of the flight. This is due to the delay charge to blow the nose cone off the rocket body to deploy the parachute. The pressure sensor is measuring local atmospheric pressure. So if there is a small explosion inside the rocket to deploy the parachute, the data will show a slight oscillation.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Altitude Data from a Rocket Launch}{pressure-7-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/altitude_rocket.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The figure above shows the pressure data converted to altitude using the equation shown above. Note that in order to get an altitude of zero at time (t=0) it is important to set \(p_0\) equal to the pressure at time (t=0). This way the altitude will be zero at the start of the flight and will increase as the rocket goes up. Again the altitude plot shows a small oscillation in altitude which isn't true but indicative of the explosion to deploy the parachute.%
\end{sectionptx}

\end{sectionptx}

\typeout{Section 23.7 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{pressure-9}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to wire up the external pressure sensor and get data from it. You need to mount the CPX\slash{}CPB and the pressure sensor to some sort of hard surface so that when you move the CPX\slash{}CPB the pressure moves as well. In order to get the pressure to change significantly you'll need to change your altitude by a lot. My recommendation is to ride the elevator of a tall building or drive up a really big hill. If you have the ability to do so, place the sensor in a rocket or an airplane!%
%

\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%

\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu showing the Plotter open showing pressure - 20\%%
\item{}Plot pressure vs time for your experiment where pressure significantly changes. - 20\%%
\item{}Convert pressure to altitude and plot altitude vs time for your experiment where altitude significantly changes. - 20\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing the pressure value in the serial Monitor in Mu. Also explain what you plan on doing to significantly change the altitude.- Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%

\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
