\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 24 Global Positioning System (GPS)}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Global Positioning System (GPS)}{}{Global Positioning System (GPS)}{}{}{gps}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{gps-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB or Arduino%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/746}{\linkhilite{GPS}} (Note that other GPS modules will work for this lab provided they output standard NMEA sentences)\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}%
\item{}Alligator Clips (x4)%
\item{}Bread Board%
\item{}Soldering iron%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{gps-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Continue to understand the concept of soldering%
\item{}Understand serial communication using TX\slash{}RX%
\item{}Learn the components of a GPS module%
\item{}Learn about standard NMEA sentences output by GPS modules%
\item{}Read GPS data and plot%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
In this lab we're going to use a GPS module to obtain location information, convert it to distance in meters and velocity.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{GPS Module without header pins soldered}{gps-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/gps.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The sensor above is the \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/746}{\linkhilite{GPS}}\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. The large square is the GPS receiver and the breakout board sends serial data via TX\slash{}RX pins to any microcontroller listening. So this is different than I2C as has been done in the previous labs with accelerometers (\hyperref[accelerometer]{\linkhilite{Subsection~{\xreffont\ref{accelerometer}}}}), IMUs (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}), or pressure sensors (\hyperref[pressure]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{pressure}}}}) but due to the high level coding it's still just as simple to parse the data . This can be purchased on Adafruit for \textdollar{}30 at the time of this writing. The interesting thing about this device is that you can actually purchase the GPS separately but this breakout board has simple \emph{TX\slash{}RX} outputs for ease of communication. The goal of this lab is not only to use this specific sensor but to understand GNSS and reinforce UART protocol.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 24.1 GPS Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{GPS Setup}{}{GPS Setup}{}{}{gps-3}
When you open the packaging of this breakout board you'll notice that the header pins are missing. First you'll need to cut a row of 9 pins and \alert{solder the header pins to the sensor}. If you're taking my class I can solder this for you or teach everyone about soldering during a lecture session of class. If you are taking this class elsewhere you have two options: try and find someone who can solder this real quick (only takes about 5 minutes) or buy your own soldering iron and try to solder yourself. Once the device is soldered you can "plug" it into a breadboard. The wiring for this system requires 4 wires. The figure below shows the GPS sensor connected to an \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-ultimate-gps/circuitpython-parsing}{\linkhilite{Adafruit Metro M0}}. (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/adafruit2}{\linkhilite{Lady Ada}} herself!)\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{GPS Wiring Diagram for Adafruit Metro M0\hyperlink{gps-wiring}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 46}]}}}{gps-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/adafruit_products_m0_express_gps_bb.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 24.2 CPB Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{CPB Wiring and Software}{}{CPB Wiring and Software}{}{}{gps-4}
The only difference between the wiring diagram shown previously and the CPX\slash{}CPB is that you will be using 4 alligator clips. The rest is straightforward. You need \emph{3.3V} to run to \emph{VIN}, \emph{GND} to \emph{GND} and then \emph{TX} to \emph{RX} and \emph{RX} to \emph{TX}. That is the \emph{TX} and \emph{RX} pins are swapped so that the transmit wire goes to the receive wire on the other device.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{GPS Module Connected to a CPB}{gps-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/gps_CPB.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the photos above and below, the red alligator clip is connected to \emph{3.3V} and is then connected to a red male-male wire which is then connected to the \emph{VIN} pin on the GPS. The black alligator clip is connected to the \emph{GND} pin which is connected to a brown male-male wire connected to the \emph{GND} pin on the GPS. The white alligator clip is connected to the \emph{TX} pin connected to an orange male-male wire connected to the \emph{RX} pin on the GPS. Finally, the yellow alligator clip is connected to the \emph{RX} pin connected to a yellow male-male wire connected to the \emph{TX} pin on the GPS.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{GPS Module Connected to a CPB with a Breadboard}{gps-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/gps.jpeg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once you have the circuit wired and soldered it's time to work on software. First, you want to make sure you have your \href{https://circuitpython.org/downloads}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python UF2}} up to date\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. Once I updated my UF2 I also updated my \href{https://circuitpython.org/libraries}{\linkhilite{Circuit Python Libraries}} (See \hyperref[installing-modules]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{installing-modules}}}} for help on installing extra modules on your CPX\slash{}CPB)\hyperlink{CircuitPython}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 30}]}}. In this lab I purposefully made the code a bit more elaborate by adding, bluetooth, and method 3 data logging. The reason is that my students in Flight Dynamics and Aircraft Design have to build a hobbyist radio controlled aircraft and get GPS data during the flight. This way they know where they flew and how fast. As such they need to build a standalone datalogger that can be placed into an airplane. The specific modules I needed for this lab for all of that functionality is as follows:%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}lis3dh%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}gps%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}thermistor%
\item{}neopixel%
\item{}adafruit\textunderscore{}ble%
\end{enumerate}
%
\par
Notice that I also added the thermistor and lis3dh libraries to get acceleration and temperature from the CPB. Once you have the necessary modules you can run some example code. Once you have all the modules, you can run the example code to \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/tree/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/GPS}{\linkhilite{log gps and acceleration data}}. Note that the link previously shown is to a folder of a few different pieces of software. The reason is because some students have a CPX that doesn't have bluetooth. Make sure to select the correct version for your hardware. Once you get it working the output will look something like this.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{GPS Data in Mu}{gps-4-8}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/GPS_No_Lock_Mu.PNG}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Notice that in the photo above the multiple columns of data are time,-99,-99,-99,0.0,x accel,y accel, z accel. The -99 values are dummy values for latitude, longitude and altitude because the GPS doesn't have a lock on any satellites. The 0.0 is the speed in knots which is also zero because there is no lock. The x,y,z accel values are from the accelerometer on the CPB. Move to \hyperref[gps-explanation]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{gps-explanation}}}} to learn about getting a lock and how to process the GPS information once you get a lock.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 24.3 Arduino Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{}{gps-5}
The wiring for the Arduino depends on which type of Arduino you're using. For every Arduino the \emph{Serial0} pins are hardwired to the USB port so you can't use them which means you need to use \emph{Serial1} or similar. The problem is that the Arduino UNO does not have any more serial ports so you have to use \emph{SoftwareSerial}. When using \emph{SoftwareSerial} you can plug the \emph{TX\slash{}RX} pins into any two digital ports and then create a \emph{SoftwareSerial} instance. If you're using an Arduino MEGA you can use any of the hardware Serial ports. In the figure below the GPS is plugged into \emph{Serial1} and thus \emph{TX} on the GPS is connected to \emph{RX1} on the Arduino MEGA and \emph{RX} on the GPS is connected to \emph{TX1} on the Arduino MEGA. The Figure below also has an IMU (\hyperref[imu]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{imu}}}}) and micro sd card breakout board (\hyperref[sdcard]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{sdcard}}}}) connected to the Arduino to build a standalone datalogger capable of measuring acceleration and GPS and log that to a micro SD card.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Hooked up to a GPS and external IMU}{gps-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_GPS_Accel_Hardware.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the Figure above, the blue wire goes from \emph{VIN} to \emph{5V} on the Arduino, the left most green wire goes from \emph{GND} to \emph{GND} while the next two green wires connect \emph{TX\slash{}RX} to \emph{RX1\slash{}TX1} respectively on the Arduino MEGA. Moving on to software also depends on the Arduino MEGA or UNO. On the UNO since you're using \emph{SoftwareSerial} you need to ensure that you have \emph{\#include \textless{}SoftwareSerial.h\textgreater{}} at the top of your code and that the \emph{SoftwareSerial} library is installed in Arduino IDE. Thankfully \emph{SoftwareSerial} is already built-in to the Arduino IDE so there's no need to download it. The code for an \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/UNO/GPS/GPS_Shield_Print/GPS_Shield_Print.ino}{\linkhilite{Arduino UNO example}} can be found on Github but will not be shown in this chapter. Notice though that on line 33 upon following that link, pins 8 and 7 are used for the \emph{SoftwareSerial} port given by this line of code: \emph{SoftwareSerial mySerial(8, 7);}. For the \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/GPS_Only/GPS_Only.ino}{\linkhilite{Arduino MEGA}}, the code is also on Github but the \emph{Serial} port is different as shown in the code below.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Code for GPS Only}{gps-5-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_MEGA_GPS_Only.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Notice, in this code the call to create the \emph{GPS} object is done using \emph{\&Serial1} rather than \emph{\&mySerial} which is an instance of the \emph{SoftwareSerial} library. While \emph{\&Serial1} is simply built-in to the Arduino MEGA software suite \emph{\&mySerial} is a specific instance of \emph{SoftwareSerial} that is only required on the Arduino UNO or similar boards that don't have Hardware Serial capabilities. However, for either the Arduino UNO or MEGA it is still required to get the \href{https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_GPS}{\linkhilite{Adafruit\textunderscore{}GPS library}} which can be downloaded from Github and installed by navigating to Sketch\textgreater{}Include Library\textgreater{}Add .ZIP Library... and then add the ZIP file you just downloaded. This library is needed to parse the specific raw GPS sentences (\hyperref[gps-explanation]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{gps-explanation}}}}) coming in. Once you've installed all the libraries, compiled the code, wired up the Arduino with the GPS and flashed the code you should be able to open the Serial Monitor with a baud rate of 115200 and see the GPS output.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial monitor output for GPS Only}{gps-5-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/GPS_Arduino_Output.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The Figure above shows the GPS output when you don't have a lock on any satellites which is why Latitude and Longitude have zeros. Still, this is a good check to make sure you're getting information correctly from the GPS since the date and time is typically accurate even without a lock. Move to \hyperref[gps-explanation]{\linkhilite{Section~{\xreffont\ref{gps-explanation}}}} to learn about getting a lock and how to process the GPS information once you get a lock. Along with getting a lock on the GPS, it's possible to augment the software to also read the external IMU from I2C and log all data to the micro SD card. A snippet of the software is shown below while the software itself for a \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/MEGA/Accelerometer_GPS_LSM6DS33_SD/Accelerometer_GPS_LSM6DS33_SD.ino}{\linkhilite{standalone GPS+IMU datalogger}} is on Github us usual.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino MEGA Code for GPS,IMU and SD card breakout boards}{gps-5-9}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_GPS_IMU_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The code shows the loop routine polling the GPS using the \emph{useInterrupt} function as well as the \emph{sensors.getEvent()} routine to poll the IMU. After compiling the code, verifying the hardware connections and flashing the software the Serial monitor will produce something similar to the code below. Again in this case the latitude and longitude are shown as zeros because there is no lock.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Serial Monitor Output for LPS22 and IMU}{gps-5-11}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_GPS_Accel_Output.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 24.4 GPS Receiver and Sentence Explanation}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{GPS Receiver and Sentence Explanation}{}{GPS Receiver and Sentence Explanation}{}{}{gps-explanation}
The GPS constellation of satellites orbiting planet Earth are around 26 satellites. Typically there are about 13 satellites above you from horizon to horizon. However, with cloud cover and buildings and trees you can typically only receive data from about 7 of them. You need at least 3 but 4 is preferred to get a GPS lock. If you go outside on a clear data and wait your data will return coordinates for your current position. Latitude is your degree value above or below the equator, longitude is your degree value east or west of the prime meridian and then altitude is your height above sea level. Note that if you're code is printing raw NMEA sentences, you may get something like this\begin{preformatted}
$GPGGA,123519,4807.038,N,01131.000,E,1,08,0.9,545.4,M,46.9,M,,*47
\end{preformatted}
This is the raw NMEA sentence output by the GPS. The code in the example folder parses this data to get the latitude, longitude, altitude and speed. The NMEA sentence is a command that contains all the GPS data. The first part is the type of sentence (GPGGA in this case) and then the rest of the data is separated by commas. The format is type of sentence, time, latitude, N\slash{}S, longitude, E\slash{}W, fix quality, number of satellites, horizontal dilution of position, altitude, M (meters), height of geoid above WGS84 ellipsoid, M (meters), time since last DGPS update, DGPS reference station id, checksum. The code in either Arduino (\emph{Adafruit\textunderscore{}GPS}) or CPX\slash{}CPB (\emph{adafruit\textunderscore{}gps}) parses this data to get the latitude, longitude, altitude and speed. The code then prints this data in a more readable format. The code also converts the latitude and longitude from the NMEA format to decimal degrees which is easier to work with. Again, once you go outside and get a lock from more than 3 satellites, the latitude and longitude values will be replaced with your actual coordinates and speed. It's possible to convert GPS position to X and Y coordinate provided you use the first GPS coordinate as the origin. The equations to convert GPS coordinates to position are shown below.%
\begin{equation}
x = R cos(\lambda)(\phi - \phi_0) \label{gps-explanation-2-4}
\end{equation}
%
\begin{equation*}
y = R (\lambda - \lambda_0)
\end{equation*}
where \(R\) is the radius of the Earth, \(\phi\) is the longitude, \(\lambda\) is the latitude and \(\lambda_0\) and \(\phi_0\) are the latitude and longitude of the origin point. In this case the x-axis is East-West and the y-axis is North-South. Once you have position you can take the derivative to get velocity typically doing a heavily filtered moving average derivative to get a smooth velocity plot. To start though a simple first order derivative will at least get you started.%
\begin{equation}
v_x = \frac{x_{i+1}-x_i}{t_{i+1}-t_i} \label{gps-explanation-2-11}
\end{equation}
%
\begin{equation*}
v_y = \frac{y_{i+1}-y_i}{t_{i+1}-t_i}
\end{equation*}
%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 24.5 Results from an aircraft flight}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Results from an aircraft flight}{}{Results from an aircraft flight}{}{}{gps-7}
My students have placed this entire data logger into an airplane and flown it. The aircraft itself was a P-51 clone made out of balsa and clear monokote.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Aircraft with my students after their maiden flight}{gps-7-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Aircraft.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The datalogger itself was soldered to a custom printed circuit board courtesy of Aker Solutions and permanently soldered to a \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4600}{\linkhilite{QtPy}}\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}. The QtPy is a super small microcontroller without any extra sensors so the students only measured GPS and nothing else.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Datalogger inside balsa airplane}{gps-7-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/GPS_Datalogger_Aircraft.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
After the flight at the ACMA (Azalea City of Model Aeronautics) airfield in Irvington, AL, the students were able to plot the GPS position of the flight. The color code indicates speed throughout the flight.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Latitude vs Longitude for Aircraft Flight}{gps-7-7}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/GPS_Flight.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
It's clear from the data that the pilot took off and landed on the runway after flying a few loops around the radio controlled flying field.%
\end{sectionptx}

\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{gps-9}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to wire up the external GPS and get data from it. You need to mount the CPX\slash{}CPB and the GPS sensor to some sort of hard surface so that when you move the CPX\slash{}CPB the GPS moves as well. In order to get a significant change in GPS coordinates I recommend going for a bike ride or a walk. If you decide to use a car I strongly recommend having a partner drive the car so you can focus on data acquisition and plotting. Once you have your GPS coordinates, convert them to position and velocity.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction with wiring diagram - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your CPX\slash{}CPB mounted to a hard surface with the GPS sensor on a breadboard. - 10\%%
\item{}Include a selfie of you right before you change location either by going for a bike ride or a walk. Explain how you plan on changing location significantly in the supporting text. - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu showing the Plotter open showing GPS data - 10\%%
\item{}Plot GPS coordinates (Latitude vs Longitude) for your experiment where coordinates significantly change. - 10\%%
\item{}Convert GPS coordinates to position and plot (X vs Y) for your experiment where position significantly changes. - 20\%%
\item{}Convert your position to velocity and plot velocity vs time for your experiment where velocity significantly changes. Comment on whether your velocity is accurate to the speed you were traveling - 20\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing your GPS getting a lock with the Serial monitor open in Mu. Also explain what you plan on doing to significantly change the GPS coordinates. - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 25 Load Cells}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Load Cells}{}{Load Cells}{}{}{load-cell}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Parts List}}{load-cell-2-1}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Laptop%
\item{}CPX\slash{}CPB or Arduino%
\item{}USB Cable%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/5974}{\linkhilite{HX711 Load Cell Amplifier}} (Note that other amplifiers will work)\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}%
\item{}\href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/5974}{\linkhilite{Strain Gauge Load Cell}} \hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}%
\item{}Alligator Clips (x4)%
\item{}Bread Board%
\item{}Soldering iron%
\item{}Digital Scale%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
\begin{listptx}{List}{\textbf{Learning Objectives}}{load-cell-2-2}{}%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Continue to understand the concept of soldering%
\item{}Load Cell calibration%
\item{}Read load cell data and plot it%
\end{enumerate}
\end{listptx}%
In this lab we're going to use a strain gauge load cell and load cell amplifier to make a digital scale.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Strain Gauge Load Cell}{load-cell-2-4}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/load_cell.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The sensor above is the \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/4543}{\linkhilite{a strain gauge load cell}}\hyperlink{AdafruitMAIN}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 34}]}}. The load cell itself is mounted to a metal beam with large holes in it so it can deflect when a load is placed on to it. The strain gauge itself changes its resistance when a load is applied but the output voltage is so low that an amplifier is needed.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Load Cell Amplifier}{load-cell-2-6}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/load_cell_amplifier.jpg}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The figure above shows the \href{https://www.adafruit.com/product/5974}{\linkhilite{HX711 load cell amplifier}} which takes the signal from the load cell and amplifies it and converts it to a nice clean digital signal similar to I2C but not quite. In this chapter we're going to wire up the load cell and then read the data from amplifier after calibrating it properly.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 25.1 Load Cell Setup}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Load Cell Setup}{}{Load Cell Setup}{}{}{load-cell-3}
When you open the packaging of the amplifier and strain gauge, you'll notice that the header pins on the amplifier are missing. First you'll need to cut a row of 6 pins and \alert{solder the header pins to the sensor}. If you're taking my class I can solder this for you or teach everyone about soldering during a lecture session of class. If you are taking this class elsewhere you have two options: try and find someone who can solder this real quick (only takes about 5 minutes) or buy your own soldering iron and try to solder yourself. Once the device is soldered you can "plug" it into a breadboard. The wiring for this system requires 4 wires from the microcontroller to the amplifier and then 4 wires from the strain gauge to the amplifier. The figure below shows the load cell and load cell amplifier connected to a \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/adafruit-nau7802-24-bit-adc-stemma-qt-qwiic/python-circuitpython}{\linkhilite{Feather M4}}. (Courtesy of \href{https://learn.adafruit.com/u/BlitzCityDIY}{\linkhilite{Liz Clark}})\hyperlink{Adafruit}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 32}]}}.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Load Cell Wiring Diagram for Adafruit Feather M4\hyperlink{load-cell-wiring}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 47}]}}}{load-cell-3-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/adafruit_products_featherBB_revC_bb.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
In the wiring diagram above, the strain gauge is connected to the amplifier with 4 wires. The red wire is connected to \emph{E+}, the black wire is connected to \emph{E-}, the white wire is connected to \emph{A-} and the green wire is connected to \emph{A+}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 25.2 CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}{}{CPX\slash{}CPB Wiring and Software}{}{}{load-cell-4}
For the CPX\slash{}CPB the strain gauge and amplifier are the same but you will be using 4 alligator clips as shown in the photo below. Although very non-standard, there are two yellow alligator clips. The first yellow alligator clip is connected to \emph{3.3V} and is then connected to a purple male-male wire which is then connected to the \emph{VIN} pin on the amplifier. The white alligator clip is connected to the \emph{GND} pin which is connected to a brown male-male wire connected to the \emph{GND} pin on the amplifier. The green alligator clip is connected to the \emph{A2} pin connected to an white male-male wire connected to the \emph{SCK} pin on the amplifier. Finally, the second yellow alligator clip is connected to the \emph{A1} pin connected to a green male-male wire connected to the \emph{DATA} pin on the amplifier.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Load Cell Amplifier connected to a CPX}{load-cell-4-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/CPX_HX711_Wiring.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
This isn't as straightforward because the amplifier in the parts list has an \emph{SCK} and \emph{DATA} line instead of \emph{SDA\slash{}SCL}. The good news is that it still uses digital output so the CPX\slash{}CPB can read it. So not quite I2C but still digital communication.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Strain Gauge Connected to a Load Cell Amplifier}{load-cell-4-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/HX711_Amplifier.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
Once you have the circuit wired there are no extra modules to install since we're just going to be using the digitalio library. The example code on Github \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Circuit_Playground/CircuitPython/HX711/hx711_mytest.py}{\linkhilite{reads and calibrates the load cell data}} and converts it to kg for ease of use. Note that kg is not a unit of weight but since we're on Earth we're assuming that we're under 1g acceleration and thus kg can be used as weight even though it's actually a mass. \begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Load Cell Output in Kg in Mu}{load-cell-4-6-2}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/HX711_Screenshot.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
 The photo above shows the example output of the load cell in kg by applying a load to the strain gauge. Using this simple script it is easy to weigh certain objects and get the value from the load cell. Notice however, that on line 73 at the time of this writing, the scale value is set to 12,000. That value will need to be adjusted based on your own calibration measurements which is left as an assignment to the reader.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 25.3 Arduino Wiring and Software}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{Arduino Wiring and Software}{}{}{load-cell-5}
The wiring for the Arduino family is extremely similar to the CPX\slash{}CPB. The figure below shows an Arduino connect to a load cell as well as many other external components that my students used for their final project.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino Connected to an HX711 plus many other components}{load-cell-5-3}{}%
\begin{image}{0.135}{0.73}{0.135}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/Arduino_HX711.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The \emph{DATA} line connects to pin 3 on the Arduino while \emph{SCK} connects to pin 2 and then \emph{5V} and \emph{GND} pins are straightforward. The \href{https://github.com/cmontalvo251/Microcontrollers/blob/master/Arduino/UNO/Load_Cell/HX711_Arduino_Example.ino}{\linkhilite{Arduino code}} is also on Github and starts with the example script from the \href{https://github.com/bogde/HX711}{\linkhilite{HX711 library}} which is also on Github. To install downloaded ZIP libraries, navigate to Sketch\textgreater{}Include Library\textgreater{}Add .ZIP Library... and then add the ZIP file downloaded from Github.%
\begin{figureptx}{Figure}{Arduino Code for the HX711}{load-cell-5-5}{}%
\begin{image}{0.125}{0.75}{0.125}{}%
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{external/HX711_Arduino_Code.png}
\end{image}%
\tcblower
\end{figureptx}%
The screenshot above shows the \emph{\#include \textless{}HX711.h\textgreater{}} on line 34 which imports the HX711 library and then the rest of the setup sets up pins 2 and 3 for the strain gauge. There are also lines of code used to setup Serial communication so that you can see the output of the scale and also change the calibration factor by pressing a and z or + and - to either inrease or decrease the scale factor.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 25.4 Assignment}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Assignment}{}{Assignment}{}{}{load-cell-6}
\begin{introduction}{}%
For this assignment you are to wire up a strain gauge and load cell amplifier and calibrate the sensor using known weights and another digital scale. First, set the scale value to zero and then place known weights on to the load cell and record the output. Place the known weight on a different calibrated scale and record those measurements as well. Repeat this experiment for 10 different known weights and plot the actual weight on the y-axis from the known digital scale and the output from the load cell on the x-axis. Use this data to compute a regression curve similar to \hyperref[servo]{\linkhilite{Chapter~{\xreffont\ref{servo}}}} and write the equation relating load cell output to actual "weight" (mass) in kg and report the scale value necessary for your code.%
%
\begin{enumerate}
\item{}Title page with name,title and date - 5\%%
\item{}Introduction with wiring diagram - 5\%%
\item{}All figures must have appropriate annotations, figure captions, figure labels and a paragraph of supporting text - 10\%%
\item{}Include a photo of your CPX\slash{}CPB connected to your amplifier and load cell. - 10\%%
\item{}Include a screenshot (entire computer screen) of Mu showing the Plotter open showing load cell data as you press on the strain gauge - 10\%%
\item{}Include a table of your known weights and load cell output in kg. - 20\%%
\item{}Include a Figure of your calibration data plotted with your trend line on top. Put your \(r^2\) value in the title of the plot - 20\%%
\item{}Write your regression equation as (\emph{actual weight = load cell output \slash{} scale}) being sure to replace scale with your actual values. Remember to round to appropriate significant figures - 10\%%
\item{}Using your calibration equation imbedded in your Mu code, place an unknown weight on your load cell and see what the output is and compare to the actual weight once you place it on a scale. Include a photo and screenshot of this experiment - 10\%%
\item{}Appendix A - Video Details - Film yourself and your entire screen showing the load cell value changing as you press on the strain gauge. - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix B - Code used to take data and code used to plot in plain text (no screenshots) - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}Appendix C - Screenshot of Excel or Sheets if applicable - Pass\slash{}Fail%
\item{}\alert{If you are asked to fix something in the Appendices such as missing content in your video or screenshots of your code, you will lose 10\% of your grade everytime you submit an update after the deadline.}%
\end{enumerate}
\end{introduction}%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}

\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 26 Results and Discussion}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Results and Discussion}{}{Results and Discussion}{}{}{results-discussion}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
The chapters above detailed about 20 projects with multiple subsections that can be used for an instrumentation course. These projects have iterated and been deployed in a real classroom environment since the Fall of 2019. The results and discussion below represent multiple years of analysis and \textquotedblleft{}in the field\textquotedblright{} iteration of courses. That is, the authors and faculty have used feedback from students to make the course better and better every semester. This chapter here represents the results and discussion of feedback from the students as well as conclusions drawn from faculty and the authors of this text.%
\end{introduction}%
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 26.1 Data Collection and Analysis}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Data Collection and Analysis}{}{Data Collection and Analysis}{}{}{results-discussion-3}
Qualitative data collection for this study was done using end of the semester surveys. Course surveys are delivered by the University Office of Institutional Effectiveness. The instructor of the course only receives results of the survey. Eight questions in the survey are restricted to a 1-5 Likert scale while four questions are open ended text responses\hyperlink{likert}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 49}]}}. The surveys are sent to every student taking the course via email. The surveys are optional and as such not all students completed the survey. The data for the course was compiled for all questions and compared against the Departmental average. This provides a base of comparison for this course across the Department of Mechanical Engineering. It is worth noting that the authors do reflect on how their own positionality may affect interpretation of data. This reflectivity is a core characteristic in qualitative research and is important in establishing credibility and showing transparency. A more in depth analysis of the results are examined in the discussion section.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 26.2 Results}
\typeout{************************************************}
%

From analysis of the surveys, 4 themes emerged which capture student perceptions of the newly developed course. These include: Teaching Style, Course Design and; Comparison, Balance between Theory and; Application and Connected Learning. \hyperref[t_themes]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{t_themes}}}} shows these four themes with definitions of each. These themes overall represent the perspectives students had on the course based on the survey results.%
\begin{tableptx}{Table}{\textbf{Resultant themes and definitions of themes}}{t_themes}{}%
\centering%
{\tabularfont%
\begin{tabular}{AcAcA}\hrulethin
\terminology{Theme}&\terminology{Definition}\tabularnewline\hrulemedium

}\tabularnewline\hrulethin

}\tabularnewline\hrulethin
Balance between Theory \& Application&\multicolumn{1}{m{0.6\linewidth}A}{\raggedright%

}\tabularnewline\hrulethin

}\tabularnewline\hrulethin

}%

\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 26.3 Discussion}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Discussion}{}{Discussion}{}{}{results-discussion-5}
This lab at home kit has been implemented since the Fall of 2019. By the time of this writing, the kit has been implemented for 7 semesters, three of which were during COVID-19. The kit was very useful during the COVID-19 pandemic (Spring 2020, Fall 2020 and Spring 2021) because students could order the kit and complete the projects remotely. If students had trouble they could email issues or take a video and share it or even set up a quick video conferencing call. The students were able to see their course objectives hands on which provided a unique way of learning during the pandemic. When the students used the kit in the classroom once the University returned to in person lectures, the students still enjoyed the projects as shown in the survey results.%
\par
In regards to the second research question about student satisfaction of the course through the use of the kit, \hyperref[tab_quotes1]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{tab_quotes1}}}} and \hyperref[tab_quotes2]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{tab_quotes2}}}} clearly show a positive response in regards to the projects and the lab at home kit. For brevity only two to three quotes were shown but for some semesters at least 25 quotes were generated with many of them reflecting on the "hands on learning" component of the course.%
\par
Furthermore, \hyperref[tab_scores]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{tab_scores}}}} shows an on average course rating that is 22\% higher than the departmental average. It can be said with confidence that the quotes in \hyperref[tab_quotes1]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{tab_quotes1}}}} and \hyperref[tab_quotes2]{\linkhilite{Table~{\xreffont\ref{tab_quotes2}}}} are in direct response to the implementation of the kit and that the students would not have written these words had the kit not been an addition to the course. This proves the first research question that a kit can be implemented as well as answers the second research question.%
\par
When these quotes are taken into consideration with the higher course ratings and higher student responses, it is evident that the take-home lab kit promoted more active student engagement in the course and, by extension, more active student learning. This data shows, in conjunction with student commentary, that the projects associated with kits fostered a positive learning experience for students while also covering required content for the course. Projects like this one are a strong strategy professors can add to their repertoire as they teach content. Per Gardner's and Bloom's theories of learning, this would indicate the students likely mastered content and skills in this setting just as well as, if not better than they would in a more traditional lecture format\hyperlink{Armstrong}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 19}]}}\hyperlink{Gardner}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 11}]}}.%
\end{sectionptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Section 26.4 Limitations of Findings}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{sectionptx}{Section}{Limitations of Findings}{}{Limitations of Findings}{}{}{results-discussion-6}
Although survey analysis resulted in themes that gave perspectives on the course, there are clear limitations in the resultant findings. The authors are well aware that this data does not conclusively show that implementing a kit in a course increases achievement of course objectives. Many other variable are present in this study, such as the instructor, the course and even the classroom the course is taught in. This study is limited in generalizability based on the small response rate in some of the semesters. Findings are also not generalizable to all engineering Instrumentation courses across the world or even the United States given the influence of the instructor's teaching style and personality in the course. Future research should expand on the study by recruiting individual interview participants and survey respondents to gain a better understanding of the impact of the course on students.%
\par
However, findings are useful for instructors seeking to improve their own course by adopting the at-home kit and documentation developed. This theme appeared multiple times among students and it is very possible that students were confused about whether they liked the course or liking the professor. For students, the instructor had a big influence on making the course enjoyable; Preliminary results from this qualitative study can be used to inform course design and teaching practice in future courses.%
\end{sectionptx}
\end{chapterptx}
%
%
\typeout{************************************************}
\typeout{Chapter 27 Conclusions}
\typeout{************************************************}
%
\begin{chapterptx}{Chapter}{Conclusions}{}{Conclusions}{}{}{conclusions}
\renewcommand*{\chaptername}{Chapter}
\begin{introduction}{}%
A lab at home kit has been described here. This kit is part of the curriculum for Instrumentation and Experimental Methods and uses the CircuitPlayground Express (CPX) from Adafruit as well as other electronic components. The CPX uses CircuitPython, a derivative of Python. Python like all scripting languages allows students to focus on writing concise code to use the CPX as a tool rather than getting bogged down in syntax, compilation and runtime errors. All software is free, open source and has numerous examples on the Adafruit Learn page as well as the internet at large and Github \hyperlink{Github}{\linkhilite{[{\xreffont 33}]}}.%
\par
As with all other courses in engineering it is possible to teach this course without any hands on material. Instead, a project based learning (PBL) style is utilized where students use the CPX and other components of the lab at home kit to complete guided projects requiring data acquisition, analysis and post-processing all geared towards enhancing learning of course objectives. The final project in the class allows the students to be creative, take ownership, collaborate with others, and use critical thinking to develop something new.%
\par
The responses from the students is positive with a 22\% increase over the department average over 7 semesters. The qualitative survey responses from students are also positive and show that the kit is effective in enhancing student satisfaction through interaction of tangible objects. Student satisfaction correlates to student performance as shown in the literature. Hopefully, similar kits can be created for other classes at USA as well as other universities in STEM across the world.%
\end{introduction}%
\end{chapterptx}
